Results. License: CC BY 4.0 arXiv:2610.02665v2 [cs.CL] 12 Nov 2026 
Large Language Continuous Diffusion Models Zhihan Yang Affiliation: NVIDIA Affiliation: Cornell University Wei Guo Affiliation: NVIDIA Affiliation: Georgia Institute of Technology † Work done during internship at NVIDIA. * Core contributors, equal contribution. ⋆ Equal supervision.Correspondence to: mmardani@nvidia.com Jean-Marie Lemercier Affiliation: NVIDIA Simon Welker Mohammad Mahdi Kamani Affiliation: NVIDIA Yonggan Fu Affiliation: NVIDIA Sajad Norouzi Affiliation: NVIDIA Julius Berner Tomas Geffner Affiliation: NVIDIA Karsten Kreis Affiliation: NVIDIA Yongxin Chen Affiliation: Georgia Institute of Technology † Work done during internship at NVIDIA. * Core contributors, equal contribution. ⋆ Equal supervision.Correspondence to: mmardani@nvidia.com Molei Tao Affiliation: Georgia Institute of Technology † Work done during internship at NVIDIA. * Core contributors, equal contribution. ⋆ Equal supervision.Correspondence to: mmardani@nvidia.com John Thickstun Pavlo Molchanov Affiliation: NVIDIA Affiliation: Cornell University Ante Jukić Affiliation: NVIDIA Arash Vahdat Affiliation: NVIDIA Morteza Mardani Affiliation: NVIDIA Abstract 
Despite the success of discrete diffusion language models (dLMs) for fast parallel decoding, their non-smooth, high-dimensional space hinders trajectory steering for reasoning and inference acceleration. To overcome this, we present Nemotron-Labs-Sigma , the first family of large-scale (3B/8B) continuous dLMs built on steerable, low-dimensional ODE/SDE latent trajectories. Trained blockwise via likelihood optimization, Nemotron-Labs-Sigma jointly denoises Gaussian-corrupted token embeddings while learning an optimal embedding geometry, and leverages pretrained autoregressive (AR) weights for warm-starting. At inference, we identify classifier-free guidance and score temperature as essential for high-fidelity reasoning and coding. Against state-of-the-art discrete counterparts (masked dLMs and AR baselines), Nemotron-Labs-Sigma outperforms them on standard benchmarks after pretraining (e.g., GSM8K, Minerva, HumanEval, MBPP) and on challenging reasoning tasks after supervised fine-tuning (e.g., MATH-500, AIME), with the largest consistent gains on coding. Beyond accuracy, we exploit two structural properties unique to continuous dLMs. First, smooth trajectories degrade gracefully at low NFEs and enable efficient few-step distillation: distribution matching proves strongest among the methods we study, giving roughly 8 × 8\times acceleration on math and 4 × 4\times on coding ( 8 8 and 4 4 tokens per forward pass) with negligible accuracy loss and preserved diversity. In addition, the one-step speculative decoding on the (pre-SFT) distilled 8B model yields a further ∼ 3.5 × \sim\!3.5\times throughput gain over the AR counterpart (on GB200), outperforming masked dLM baselines. Second, embedding-space steering controls the quality–diversity trade-off, yielding strong pass@ k k that keeps improving as k k grows. Together, these results establish continuous dLMs as a promising paradigm for efficient language generation. Model family weights are publicly available at Nemotron-Labs-Sigma . 
1 Introduction 
While diffusion language models (dLMs) enable fast parallel decoding compared to autoregressive (AR) baselines ( Inception Labs et al., 2025 ; Song et al., 2025 ; Fu et al., 2026 ; Zhu et al., 2026 ; DiffusionGemma Team et al., 2026 ) , dominant discrete dLMs operate in high-dimensional, categorical state spaces (e.g., vocabulary size V ≥ 100 ​ k V\geq 100\text{k} ) that suffer under limited step budgets and impede trajectory control for complex reasoning and coding tasks. Continuous diffusion offers a principled way to overcome these structural limitations through smooth latent geometries that natively support structured editing ( Meng et al., 2022 ) , flexible ODE trajectory steering ( Zhang and Chen, 2023 ; Lu et al., 2022 ; Zhang et al., 2023 ) , and efficient few-step distillation ( Salimans and Ho, 2022 ; Song et al., 2023 ) . 
Recently, there has been growing interest in continuous diffusion and flow matching for text generation ( Gulrajani and Hashimoto, 2023 ; Dieleman et al., 2022 ; Chen et al., 2026 ; Hu et al., 2026 ; Lee et al., 2026 ; Guo et al., 2026 ; Liang et al., 2026 ; Yang et al., 2026 ) ; see App. A for a review. However, existing methods remain limited in scale ( < 2 ​ B <2\text{B} parameters) or focus on unconditional generation, lacking evaluations on complex reasoning tasks. Whether continuous dLMs scale to complex conditional reasoning remains open. This is a non-trivial hurdle because continuous models update block embeddings globally, in contrast to the factorized categorical denoising of discrete dLMs that leads to semi-autoregressive token commitment per block. 
To overcome these limitations, we present Nemotron-Labs-Sigma (3B/8B): s cal i ng up G aussian diffusion m odels for l a nguage. For brevity, we refer to the model family simply as Sigma throughout the remainder of the paper. We propose a family of continuous dLMs operating over a 16-dimensional embedding space trained blockwise via likelihood optimization. Within each block, the model learns to jointly denoise Gaussian-corrupted token embeddings and to structure an optimal embedding geometry. To scale training, we warm-start the model from existing pretrained AR weights. For inference, we explore diffusion trajectory sampling, identifying embedding-space Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) and Score Temperature (ST) as crucial ingredients for high-fidelity reasoning and coding. Figure 1 : Left: Inference pipeline. Sigma generates text blockwise via iterative diffusion denoising over 16D continuous latents ( 𝐳 t \mathbf{z}_{t} ) conditioned on a KV-cached prefix ( 𝐜 \mathbf{c} ), followed by categorical readout. Predicted vocabulary probabilities 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t , 𝐜 ) \mathbf{x}_{\theta}(\mathbf{z}_{t},t;\mathbf{c}) map to mean predictions 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t , 𝐜 ) \mathbf{e}_{\theta}(\mathbf{z}_{t},t;\mathbf{c}) in 16D space via categorical reparameterization. Clean embeddings (dots) lie on the 16D unit sphere, while 𝐞 θ \mathbf{e}_{\theta} lies on or within it. Middle: Smooth trajectory enables low-NFE reasoning. GSM8K accuracy vs. NFEs using DDIM sampler for Sigma ​​(- unreg )-8B, and sampler 17 for Sigma - distill -8B. Right: Scaling inference-time compute. pass@ k k vs. k k on MBPP (solid curves: fixed config across k k ; thin curves: alternative tested configs). 
Our key contributions are summarized as follows: 
• 
First Large-Scale Continuous dLM: We present Nemotron-Labs-Sigma (3B/8B), efficiently trained by warm-starting from pretrained AR weights, and optimizing a regularized continuous diffusion ELBO. 
• 
Competitive Math & Coding: Across six base and ten instruct benchmarks, Sigma outperforms state-of-the-art discrete dLMs/ARs on reasoning and coding. 
• 
Inference Steering (CFG & ST): By elucidating the inference design space, we show that embedding-space CFG and ST effectively govern the quality-diversity trade-off with minimal latency overhead via single-pass block-causal attention packing. 
• 
Distillation: We study trajectory- and distribution-matching distillation (DMD) methods and find that phased DMD delivers the strongest acceleration, speeding up inference by 8 × 8\times for math and 4 × 4\times for coding, reaching the equivalent of 8 and 4 tokens per forward (TPFs), respectively. 
• 
Speculative Decoding: We apply speculative decoding to both distilled and non-distilled Sigma variants, and find that combining distillation with one-step speculative decoding is highly effective, yielding a ∼ 3 × \sim\!3\times throughput improvement over AR on an NVIDIA GB200 GPU. 
2 Background Notation. 
Let 𝐱 {\mathbf{x}} be a sequence of length L L over a vocabulary 𝒱 \mathcal{V} of size V V . We identify 𝐱 {\mathbf{x}} with a matrix in { 0 , 1 } L × V \{0,1\}^{L\times V} whose rows 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 L {\mathbf{x}}^{1},...,{\mathbf{x}}^{L} are one-hot vectors in ℝ V \mathbb{R}^{V} . Δ V \Delta^{V} denotes the probability simplex over 𝒱 \mathcal{V} . ⟨ ⋅ , ⋅ ⟩ \langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle denotes the inner product. NELBO stands for negative evidence lower bound. Plaid. 
Continuous dLMs lift discrete tokens into a low-dimensional Euclidean space and run Gaussian diffusion. There have been multiple proposed design choices, see App. A for an overview.
We adopt the embedding and diffusion process from Plaid ( Gulrajani and Hashimoto, 2023 ) , a Variational Diffusion Model (VDM, Kingma et al. (2021) ) over token embeddings.
Let 𝐄 ∈ ℝ V × d e {\mathbf{E}}\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times d_{e}} be a learnable embedding matrix whose rows lie on the unit sphere, with d e ≪ V d_{e}\ll V .
The embedding of 𝐱 {\mathbf{x}} is given by 𝐞 := 𝐱𝐄 ∈ ℝ L × d e {\mathbf{e}}:={\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{E}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{e}} (i.e., standard embedding lookup). The forward process applies variance-preserving Gaussian noise on 𝐞 {\mathbf{e}} : 
q ⁡ ( 𝐳 t ∣ 𝐱 ) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( α t ​ 𝐞 , σ t 2 ​ 𝐈 ) , α t , σ t > 0 , α t 2 + σ t 2 = 1 , t ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] , \displaystyle q({\mathbf{z}}_{t}\mid{\mathbf{x}})=\mathcal{N}(\alpha_{t}{\mathbf{e}},\ \sigma_{t}^{2}{\mathbf{I}}),\qquad\alpha_{t},\sigma_{t}>0,\qquad\alpha_{t}^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}=1,\qquad t\in[0,1], (1) 
with SNR ⁡ ( t ) = α t 2 / σ t 2 \operatorname{SNR}(t)=\alpha_{t}^{2}/\sigma_{t}^{2} decreasing monotonically over t ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] t\in[0,1] . A denoiser 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t ) ∈ ( Δ V ) L ⊂ ℝ L × V {\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t)\in(\Delta^{V})^{L}\subset\mathbb{R}^{L\times V} with output prior returns a categorical distribution over the vocabulary at every position. The reverse process is the exact Gaussian posterior q ⁡ ( 𝐳 s ∣ 𝐳 t , 𝐱 ) q({\mathbf{z}}_{s}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t},{\mathbf{x}}) ( Kingma et al., 2021 , App. A.2) with 𝐱 {\mathbf{x}} replaced by 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t ) {\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t) . Its NELBO splits into 3 terms: prior KL ℒ prior \mathcal{L}_{\text{prior}} , reconstruction loss ℒ rec \mathcal{L}_{\text{rec}} , and diffusion loss ℒ diff \mathcal{L}_{\text{diff}} :

where p ⁡ ( 𝐳 1 ) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 ) p({\mathbf{z}}_{1})=\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{I}}) and the categorical reparameterization 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t ) := 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t ) ​ 𝐄 ∈ ℝ L × d e {\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t):={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t){\mathbf{E}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{e}} predicts the posterior mean embedding 𝔼 [ 𝐞 ∣ 𝐳 t , t ] \E[{\mathbf{e}}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t},t] corresponding to 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t ) {\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t) . Learned noise schedule. 
The schedule admits γ ⁡ ( t ) = γ 0 + ( γ 1 − γ 0 ) ​ γ ~ ​ ( t ) \gamma(t)=\gamma_{0}+(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{0})\tilde{\gamma}(t) with learnable endpoints γ 0 < γ 1 \gamma_{0}<\gamma_{1} and a monotone interior shape γ ~ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \tilde{\gamma}\in[0,1] , giving α t 2 = σ ⁡ ( − γ ⁡ ( t ) ) \alpha_{t}^{2}=\sigma(-\gamma(t)) , σ t 2 = σ ⁡ ( γ ⁡ ( t ) ) \sigma_{t}^{2}=\sigma(\gamma(t)) and SNR ⁡ ( t ) = e − γ ⁡ ( t ) \operatorname{SNR}(t)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma(t)} . 1 1 1 σ ⁡ ( t ) = 1 / ( 1 + e − t ) \sigma(t)=1/(1+{\mathrm{e}}^{-t}) is the sigmoid function. The diffusion term depends on the endpoints alone and is invariant to γ ~ ​ ( t ) \tilde{\gamma}(t) , which instead controls its Monte-Carlo (MC) variance ( Kingma et al., 2021 ) , so
they are learned for different reasons. Learning the interior-shape distributes denoising difficulty over t t ( Yang et al., 2026 ) . Learned embeddings. 
The
NELBO 2 depends on 𝐄 {\mathbf{E}} via 1 , where embeddings become likelihood-trained parameters, which separates Plaid from continuous dLMs built on fixed (e.g., one-hot)
encodings. Not learning embeddings significantly worsens Plaid’s performance ( Yang et al., 2026 ) . Self-conditioning (SC). 
Proposed in Chen et al. (2023) , SC lets the denoiser see its own previous estimate. Let 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t , 𝐞 sc ) {\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t;{\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}}) denote a denoiser taking an additional predicted clean embedding 𝐞 sc ∈ ℝ L × d e {\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{e}} , and 𝟎 {\mathbf{0}} for the empty condition (omitted by default in our notation). During training, 25 % 25\% of training samples are self-conditioned: a first pass without gradient tracking produces 𝐞 sc := 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t , 𝟎 ) ​ 𝐄 {\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}}:={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t;{\mathbf{0}}){\mathbf{E}} , and only the second pass, 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t , sg ⁡ ( 𝐞 sc ) ) ​ 𝐄 {\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t;\operatorname{sg}({\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}})){\mathbf{E}} , receives gradients. The remaining samples use 𝟎 {\mathbf{0}} as SC input, so the denoiser learns both branches. During validation, all samples enable SC. Since 𝐞 sc {\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}} is itself a function of ( 𝐳 t , t ) ({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t) , the NELBO remains valid. Notice that during training, 25 % 25\% SC increases the non-embedding FLOPs by only about 8 % 8\% , which is minimal. Furthermore, during inference, one directly feeds the previous-step’s prediction as SC, which incurs no overhead. Scaling. 
RePlaid ( Yang et al., 2026 ) realigns Plaid with the architectures of modern
discrete dLMs/ARs and finds that, once aligned, continuous diffusion scales competitively with masked diffusion. 
3 Large Language Continuous Diffusion Models (a) Training procedure of Sigma . (b) Attention mask for training. Figure 2 : Training procedure of Sigma (a) and its transformer attention mask (b) for an example sequence 𝐱 {\mathbf{x}} of length L = 6 L=6 with block size L ′ = 2 L^{\prime}=2 .
During pretraining, we feed the clean discrete tokens 𝐱 \mathbf{x} to the AR stream (a, left part) and the noisy continuous embeddings 𝐳 \mathbf{z} to the diffusion stream (a, right part).
For the diffusion stream, each block is randomly chosen to either compute the reconstruction loss (1st block, t = 0 t=0 ) or the diffusion loss (2nd & 3rd blocks, t ∼ Unif ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] t\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1] ); see 5 .
Reconstruction loss is the cross entropy between the logits and the ground truth tokens, while for diffusion loss we use categorical reparameterization to obtain the mean embedding prediction and compute the mean squared error against the ground truth diffusion embeddings. 
3.1 Blockwise Probabilistic Model 
Inspired by Nemotron-Labs-Diffusion (NLD, Fu et al. (2026) ), a state-of-the-art open-sourced masked dLM, we adopt a blockwise formulation for our continuous diffusion language model: the sequence 𝐱 {\mathbf{x}} is partitioned into B B contiguous blocks { 𝐱 ( b ) } b = 1 B \{{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b}=1}^{B} of size L ′ = L / B L^{\prime}=L/B , where the model denoises one block at a time given its clean prefix. As in Arriola et al. (2025) , we factorize the likelihood autoregressively across blocks and then bound each factor: 
− log ⁡ p θ ​ ( 𝐱 ) \displaystyle-\log p_{\theta}({\mathbf{x}}) = ∑ b = 1 B − log p θ ( 𝐱 ( b ) ∣ 𝐱 ( < b ) ) ≤ ∑ b = 1 B ℒ NELBO ( 𝐱 ( b ) ; 𝐱 ( < b ) ) = : ℒ BD ( 𝐱 ) , \displaystyle={\textstyle\sum}_{b=1}^{B}-\log p_{\theta}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})})\leq{\textstyle\sum}_{b=1}^{B}\mathcal{L}_{\text{NELBO}}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})};{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})})=:\mathcal{L}_{\text{BD}}({\mathbf{x}}), (3) 
where we obtain ℒ NELBO ​ ( 𝐱 ( b ) , 𝐱 ( < b ) ) \mathcal{L}_{\text{NELBO}}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})};{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})}) by conditioning the denoiser on 𝐱 ( < b ) {\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})} as follows: 
ℒ NELBO ​ ( 𝐱 ( b ) , 𝐱 ( < b ) ) \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{NELBO}}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})};{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})}) = KL ( q ( 𝐳 1 ( b ) ∣ 𝐱 ( b ) ) ∥ p ( 𝐳 1 ( b ) ) ) \displaystyle=\operatorname{KL}(q({\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})})\,\|\,p({\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})})) 
+ 𝔼 q ⁡ ( 𝐳 0 ( b ) ∣ 𝐱 ( b ) ) [ ∑ l = 1 L ′ − log ⟨ 𝐱 θ l ( 𝐳 0 ( b ) , 0 ; 𝐱 ( < b ) ) , 𝐱 ( b , l ) ⟩ ] \displaystyle+\E_{q({\mathbf{z}}_{0}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})})}\big[{\textstyle\sum}_{l=1}^{L^{\prime}}-\log\langle{\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}^{l}({\mathbf{z}}_{0}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},0;{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})}),{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b,l})}\rangle\big] 
− 1 2 ​ 𝔼 t ∼ Unif ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] ​ 𝔼 q ⁡ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) ∣ 𝐱 ( b ) ) ​ [ SNR ′ ⁡ ( t ) ​ ‖ 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐱 ( < b ) ) − 𝐞 ( b ) ‖ 2 ] . \displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\E_{t\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]}\E_{q({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\mid{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})})}[\operatorname{SNR}^{\prime}(t)\|{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})})-{\mathbf{e}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\|^{2}]. (4) Training. 
The reconstruction loss at t = 0 t=0 and diffusion loss at t ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) t\in(0,1) do not share a single forward pass. RePlaid evaluates one of them per 𝐱 ( b ) {\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} via Horvitz–Thompson estimator ( Horvitz and Thompson, 1952 ) with R ∼ Ber ⁡ ( p rec ) R\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{\text{rec}}) , i.e., 𝐱 ( b ) {\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} is assigned to ℒ rec \mathcal{L}_{\text{rec}} w.p. p rec ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p_{\text{rec}}\in(0,1) : 
ℒ NELBO ​ ( 𝐱 ( b ) , 𝐱 ( < b ) ) = 𝔼 R ∼ Ber ⁡ ( p rec ) ⁡ [ ℒ prior ​ ( 𝐱 ( b ) ) + R p rec ​ ℒ rec ​ ( 𝐱 ( b ) , 𝐱 ( < b ) ) + 1 − R 1 − p rec ​ ℒ diff ​ ( 𝐱 ( b ) , 𝐱 ( < b ) ) ] . \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{NELBO}}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})};{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})})=\E_{R\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{\text{rec}})}\big[\mathcal{L}_{\text{prior}}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})})+\tfrac{R}{p_{\text{rec}}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{rec}}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})};{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})})+\tfrac{1-R}{1-p_{\text{rec}}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{diff}}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})};{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})})\big]. (5) 
Notice that this is an unbiased estimator if ℒ diff \mathcal{L}_{\text{diff}} and ℒ rec \mathcal{L}_{\text{rec}} are unbiased. We set p rec = 0.1 p_{\text{rec}}=0.1 , empirically found to minimize the training objective variance of RePlaid. 
To construct the unbiased estimator for ℒ BD ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathcal{L}_{\text{BD}}({\mathbf{x}}) in 3 for training, we sample R ( b ) R^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} i.i.d. for each block. If R ( b ) = 0 R^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}=0 , we sample t ( b ) t^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} and 𝐳 t ( b ) ( b ) {\mathbf{z}}_{t^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} ; otherwise, we set t = 0 t=0 and sample 𝐳 0 ( b ) {\mathbf{z}}_{0}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} . The noise schedule endpoints are learned to optimize ℒ NELBO ​ ( 𝐱 ( b ) , 𝐱 ( < b ) ) \mathcal{L}_{\text{NELBO}}({\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})};{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})}) while the schedule interior shape is learned to optimize its MC variance, with both shared across blocks. As mentioned before, we apply SC only to 25 % 25\% of samples (full sequence with all blocks). See Alg. 1 for the training algorithm. 
3.2 Pretraining 
NLD utilizes AR initialization and regularization to enhance downstream performance ( Fu et al., 2026 ) . However, adapting these techniques to continuous dLMs like Sigma is non-trivial, as the activation dynamics of continuous and discrete models differ fundamentally; it is thus unclear whether discrete AR initialization remains as effective for continuous representation space. To establish a rigorous baseline, we first align Sigma ’s data, architecture, and optimization with discrete dLMs for a controlled comparison at scale ( Sec. 3.2.1 ). In Sec. 3.2.2 , we evaluate the impact of AR initialization when pretraining Sigma - unreg -3B 2 2 2 “ unreg ” means no regularization loss for the diffusion embedding 𝐄 {\mathbf{E}} . This additional loss will be introduced in Sec. 4.3 . with 3 , followed by an investigation of AR regularization for training our flagship 3B/8B models. (a) From scratch + no AR reg. Block size: 64 (b) From AR init. + no AR reg. Block size: 64 (c) From AR init. + AR reg. Block size: 32 Figure 3 : Pretraining Sigma - unreg -3B : (a) from scratch, (b) from AR initialization, and (c) from AR initialization with AR regularization. PPL for AR and diffusion streams is computed via ℒ ar ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathcal{L}_{\text{ar}}({\mathbf{x}}) in 6 and ℒ BD ​ ( 𝐱 ) \mathcal{L}_{\text{BD}}({\mathbf{x}}) in 3 , respectively. Early investigations (a, b) use block size 64; our flagship models (c) use block size 32 to match the inference block size of NLD. Proper AR initialization/regularization leads to the best Sigma model. 3.2.1 Unified Pretraining of Continuous and Discrete dLMs Data. 
As with NLD, we adopt the open-sourced pretraining data from Basant et al. (2025) . We use the Mistral-NeMo tokenizer ( V = 131,072 V=131{,}072 ) ( Mistral AI, 2024 ) , batch size 512 512 , and L = 4096 L=4096 . Architecture. 
Sigma adopts the transformer backbone of NLD and its AR baseline, ensuring the three families are parameter- and FLOP-matched. As with NLD, Sigma uses a block causal attention mask ( Fig. 2(b) ). Sigma uniquely adds a unit-norm 16-dimensional diffusion embedding table with a linear up-projection, a categorical reparameterization mapping V V -dimensional probabilities to low-dimensional soft embeddings, a self-conditioning linear projection, Fourier time conditioning, and a learnable noise schedule (see Sec. 2 ). Together, these account for less than 1 % 1\% of the total parameters. We provide thorough details in App. C . Training. 
We train our models for 150k steps, following the optimization procedure detailed in App. C . Given a batch size of 512, 150k steps correspond to over 300B tokens. Training consumed ∼ 9,600 \sim\!9{,}600 and ∼ 19,200 \sim\!19{,}200 NVIDIA GB200 GPU hours for the 3B and 8B models, respectively. 3.2.2 AR Initialization and Regularization AR Initialization. 
To evaluate the impact of initialization, we compare pretraining Sigma ( i ) from scratch and ( ii ) from an AR baseline pretrained on 1T tokens. Due to the dimension mismatch between high-dimensional AR embeddings and Sigma ’s 16-dimensional space, we randomly initialize the diffusion embedding layer. As shown in Figs. 3(a) and 3(b) , Sigma - unreg -3B ( L ′ = 64 L^{\prime}=64 ) with self-conditioning achieves competitive perplexity relative to NLD-3B ( L ′ = 64 L^{\prime}=64 ) under both regimes ( i ) and ( ii ). However, AR initialization significantly accelerates convergence and yields lower final training loss. AR Regularization. 
Continuous diffusion training inherently suffers from high gradient variance and slow convergence. To mitigate variance and accelerate optimization, we follow NLD by adding AR regularization, optimizing the joint objective ℒ ar + w diff ​ ℒ BD \mathcal{L}_{\text{ar}}+w_{\text{diff}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{BD}} ( Fig. 3(c) ; see App. C for the w diff w_{\text{diff}} weighting schedule), where 
ℒ ar = ∑ l = 2 L − log ⟨ 𝐱 θ l ( 𝐱 < l ) , 𝐱 l ⟩ \mathcal{L}_{\text{ar}}={\textstyle\sum}_{l=2}^{L}-\log\langle{\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}^{l}({\mathbf{x}}^{<l}),{\mathbf{x}}^{l}\rangle (6) 
is computed over the AR stream using causal attention ( Fig. 2 ). This architecture employs two distinct readout heads, with the AR regularization computed exclusively from the AR head. While this dual-head formulation improves auxiliary AR decoding capability, our primary objective remains maximizing diffusion decoding performance. 
Finally, input sequences utilize AR embeddings for the AR stream and 16-dimensional diffusion embeddings for the diffusion stream ( Fig. 2 ), facilitating AR initialization and efficient KV-caching. As shown in Fig. 3(c) , AR regularization significantly improves training stability; with a properly tuned w diff w_{\text{diff}} , it even outperforms pure perplexity optimization for the diffusion stream. This confirms that AR regularization stabilizes training while yielding a higher-quality model. Consequently, we adopt AR initialization, AR regularization, and a block size of L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 for all subsequent Sigma experiments. 
4 Language Generation via Continuous Diffusion Sampling 
Drawing on the success of continuous diffusion samplers using SDE/ODE solvers ( Song et al., 2021b ) , we systematically explore decoding samplers for continuous dLMs, evaluating performance across standard mathematical reasoning and coding benchmarks. 
4.1 Block Diffusion Sampling 
To generate a response 𝐫 {\mathbf{r}} given a prompt 𝐩 {\mathbf{p}} , we denoise one block at a time.
Let 𝐜 := ( 𝐩 , 𝐫 ( < b ) ) {\mathbf{c}}:=({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{r}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})}) be the clean context available when generating block b b , stored in an attention key-value (KV) cache using the AR embeddings.
An offset block-causal mask determines who can see whom ( Fig. 5(a) ).
Positions in 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} use causal attention, so their KV can be cached and reused across diffusion steps, regardless of whether the prompt length is a multiple of L ′ L^{\prime} . Positions in the noisy block 𝐳 t ( b ) {\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} attend bidirectionally within the block and causally to the entire prefix 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} . After a block is decoded, the AR embeddings of its committed tokens are appended to the causal KV cache for the next block during the next block’s first diffusion step, at no extra NFE. 
Sampling block b b starts from 𝐳 1 ( b ) ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 ) {\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\sim\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{I}}) and steps from t t to s < t s<t along a discretization of [ 1 , 0 ] [1,0] .
At each step the denoiser yields a predicted clean embedding 𝐞 ^ θ := 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 ) = 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 ) ​ 𝐄 \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}:={\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}})={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}}){\mathbf{E}} and the implied noise prediction ϵ ^ θ := 1 σ t ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) − α t ​ 𝐞 ^ θ ) \hat{\bm{\epsilon}}_{\theta}:=\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}-\alpha_{t}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}) .
We introduce two canonical samplers for the reverse process.
The DDPM sampler uses the ancestral kernel of VDM ( Kingma et al., 2021 ) . It draws 𝐳 s ( b ) {\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} from the closed-form Gaussian posterior q ⁡ ( 𝐳 s ( b ) ∣ 𝐳 t ( b ) , 𝐱 ( b ) ) q({\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\mid{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}) with the clean embedding replaced by 𝐞 ^ θ \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta} .
The DDIM sampler ( Song et al., 2021a ) leverages the predictions 𝐞 ^ θ \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta} and ϵ ^ θ \hat{\bm{\epsilon}}_{\theta} to deterministically update the state as 𝐳 s ( b ) = α s ​ 𝐞 ^ θ + σ s ​ ϵ ^ θ {\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}=\alpha_{s}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}+\sigma_{s}\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}_{\theta} .
For both samplers, after the final step, tokens are read out as argmax \argmax of the conditional logit output 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 0 ( b ) , 0 , 𝐜 ) {\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{0}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},0;{\mathbf{c}}) and appended to 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} , so NFEs = steps + 1 \text{NFEs}=\text{steps}+1 .
See Alg. 2 for the complete sampling algorithms and Sec. E.1 for more details. 
4.2 Trajectory Steering 
To sharpen model predictions, we incorporate two reverse-process steering methods to our blockwise continuous diffusion model with no impact to its training efficiency and minimal impact to its inference efficiency. Classifier-free guidance (CFG). 
CFG ( Ho and Salimans, 2022 ) is the standard tool for class-conditional or text-to-image diffusion models. In CFG, one queries the model twice to obtain a conditional and an unconditional prediction and then linearly extrapolates them.
For our block diffusion model, the clean context 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} serves as the natural conditioning information, and CFG extrapolates away from the unconditional prediction for the current block b {\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b} : 
𝐞 θ w ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 ) := 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , ∅ ) + w ⁡ [ 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 ) − 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , ∅ ) ] , \displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}^{w}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}}):={\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;\varnothing)+w\,\big[{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}})-{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;\varnothing)\big], (CFG) 
where ∅ \varnothing drops the entire clean context 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} (so 𝐳 t ( b ) {\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} attends only within its own block) and w ≥ 1 w\geq 1 is the guidance strength ( w = 1 w=1 falls back to the plain conditional prediction).
We bring two technical considerations. First, at batch size 1 1 transformer inference is memory-bound, so we pack conditional and unconditional predictions into a single forward pass at almost no wall-clock cost ( Liu et al., 2026b ) , using the packed mask of Fig. 5(a) . We measure this overhead to be less than 5 % 5\% per forward pass at block size 32 32 and 64 64 ( Sec. E.9 ).
Second, unlike in image diffusion models and existing dLMs that require train-time context dropout ( Potaptchik et al., 2026 ; Hu et al., 2026 ) , our CFG is a pure inference-time trick : due to sequence packing in pretraining, the first block typically starts in the middle of a document and naturally sees no context.
We discuss existing approaches in App. A . Score temperature (ST). 
ST ( Dieleman et al., 2022 ) is a heuristic that sharpens the conditional score itself, rescaling the score ∇ log ⁡ p t ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) ∣ 𝐜 ) \nabla\log p_{t}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}\mid{\mathbf{c}}) (or equivalently, the noise prediction) by 1 τ \frac{1}{\tau} for τ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \tau\in(0,1] . One can show that it corresponds to the following operation on the mean prediction: 
𝐞 θ τ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 ) := 𝐳 t ( b ) α t + 1 τ ​ [ 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 ) − 𝐳 t ( b ) α t ] . \displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}^{\tau}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}}):=\tfrac{{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}}{\alpha_{t}}+\tfrac{1}{\tau}\big[{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}})-\tfrac{{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}}{\alpha_{t}}\big]. (ST) Figure 4 : CFG and ST tuning on Sigma - unreg -3B for DDPM sampling. Combining both techniques yields larger gains over applying either in isolation, and markedly outperforms sampling without CFG or ST. See Fig. 15 for the full tuning grid. 
Intuitively, since 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝐳 t ( b ) | 𝐱 ( b ) ] = α t ​ 𝐞 ( b ) \E[{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}|{\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})}]=\alpha_{t}{\mathbf{e}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} , 1 α t ​ 𝐳 t ( b ) \frac{1}{\alpha_{t}}{\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} serves as the naive bad prediction to extrapolate from.
ST does not incur further model queries, and τ = 1 \tau=1 leaves the sampler unchanged. We also recognize ST as a special case of Temporal Score Rescaling proposed in Xu et al. (2026) . (a) Packed attention mask while generating block b b ( L ′ = 2 L^{\prime}=2 ). Prefix 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} is causal (KV-cached). Conditional 𝐳 t ( b ) {\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{(b)} attends to all of 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} and bidirectionally within the block; unconditional copy attends only to itself. (b) One reverse step t → s t\to s . CFG and ST mix the two denoisers into 𝐞 θ w , τ {\mathbf{e}}^{w,\tau}_{\theta} ; an update yields 𝐳 s ( b ) {\mathbf{z}}_{s}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} . SC (red) feeds the previous conditional mean (not sharpened by CFG or ST) to the next conditional branch. Figure 5 : Inference for blockwise continuous diffusion: packed CFG attention (a) and one reverse step (b). 
Finally, we remark that due to the commutativity of CFG and ST , we can combine them into a single operation 𝐞 θ w , τ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 ) {\mathbf{e}}^{w,\tau}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}}) , which is essentially using the score of the composed distribution ( p t ( ⋅ ∣ 𝐜 ) w p t ( ⋅ ∣ ∅ ) 1 − w ) 1 / τ (p_{t}(\cdot\mid{\mathbf{c}})^{w}p_{t}(\cdot\mid\varnothing)^{1-w})^{1/\tau} . We observed that using either knob is weaker than using both of them, see Fig. 4 . Self-conditioning (SC). 
At inference, SC is applied only to the conditional prediction: when querying the model at time s s , the previous step’s conditional prediction 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 ) {\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}}) (before applying CFG and ST) serves as the SC input for the conditional prediction, while the unconditional prediction receives 𝟎 {\mathbf{0}} as the SC input ( Fig. 5(b) ). Sharpening in logit space. 
While in the above, we apply guidance directly in the embedding domain, we can also sharpen in logit space.
In Sec. E.6 , we show that the logit-space CFG and temperature do provide sharpening, but the embedding-space knobs provide stronger performance, hence we keep CFG and ST as the default option. 
4.3 Embedding Regularization 
We observe that during training, embeddings collapse to approximately 5 effective dimensions (i.e., > 90 % {>}90\% explained variance in the first 5 principal components), a behavior typically not observed in AR language model embeddings but consistent with observations in RePlaid ( Yang et al., 2026 ) .
This may arise because constant embeddings are a trivial solution for the denoising loss.
We hypothesize that this embedding collapse exacerbates token ambiguity under prediction errors from coarse numerical precision and low-NFE sampling, which motivates regularizing the embeddings toward a uniform distribution on the unit sphere.
For our regularized model Sigma , we therefore apply a multi-scale extension of Sketched Uniform Spherical Regularization (SUSReg, Nicollier et al. (2026) ) to the embedding matrix 𝐄 {\mathbf{E}} during pretraining, penalizing deviations from the uniform distribution on the unit sphere.
We also add a local repulsion loss to push apart very close embedding vectors, and make other minor training changes, see Sec. C.1 for full details.
We ablate a simpler variant as in the original SUSReg ( Nicollier et al., 2026 ) , denoted Sigma - preg , which also avoids embedding collapse but is less effective at improving low-NFE quality and distillation ( Tabs. 10 and 11 ); we
report its full benchmark results in Tab. 6 . Figure 6 : Left: Pass@ k k accuracy on MBPP for Sigma -3B and Sigma - distill -3B . All models use pass@1 selected sampler. Right: Pass@ k k accuracy on AIME 2025 for Sigma -3B-Instruct , highlighting best CFG/ST pair chosen from five pre-defined combinations. Results. 
As shown in Tabs. 1 and 2 , the resulting regularized models Sigma -3B/8B achieve the highest average reasoning scores, outperforming NLD ( Fu et al., 2026 ) . Furthermore, they significantly improve few-step sampling (see Tab. 10 ) and distillation (see Sec. F.3 ) compared to Sigma - unreg -3B/8B, supporting our hypotheses.
Despite exhibiting substantially different embedding geometries from the unregularized models ( Fig. 10 ), interestingly we find that regularization incurs almost no validation PPL penalty.

See Sec. E.3 for details on the harness, scoring pipeline, error bar conventions, and the samplers for baselines, and Sec. E.2 for time discretization schemes. Results. 
In AR mode, Sigma - unreg -3B attains the best six-task average across all methods; in diffusion mode, our DDPM-256 runs are comparable and out-perform NLD-3B on average and sets the best base-model numbers on GSM8K, HumanEval, and HumanEval+; they also outperform LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B by a noticeable margin, despite being ∼ 2 × \sim\!2\times smaller in parameters. Empirically, we observe less left-to-right bias within a block for Sigma compared with the confidence-based masked dLM sampling ( Fig. 19 ). To confirm the benefit of AR regularization ( Sec. 3.2.2 ), we also trained Sigma - unreg -3B with ℒ BD \mathcal{L}_{\text{BD}} alone 3 without ℒ ar \mathcal{L}_{\text{ar}} 6 ; results are uniformly worse across all tasks ( Fig. 16 ). We refer readers to Tab. 2 for the same evaluation on 8B base models. Pass@ k k analysis. 
While pass@ 1 1 accuracy in Tab. 1 captures single-draw quality, test-time search and RL draw multiple rollouts for the same prompt, where diverse rollouts are needed for richer learning signals and increase the chance of discovering a correct solution. Pass@ k k does not directly measure diversity, but tests whether additional samples expand correct-solution coverage.
We report results in Fig. 6 on MBPP at the 3B scale, using the same sampler configuration that performs best on pass@ 1 1 accuracy with 256 NFEs DDIM sampler.
There, Sigma -3B increasingly surpasses as more trials are available, showing that the model has a useful output diversity.
See Sec. E.8 for the full study.

Full results on base tasks in Tab. 10 . Figure 8 : Pass@ 1 1 accuracy of Sigma - unreg -8B, Sigma -8B and Sigma - distill -8B. Undistilled models use DDIM with best per-NFE (CFG, ST), shared across the three tasks.
Full results on base tasks in Tab. 11 . 
5.1 ODE Sampler and Time Discretization 
In previous results, we mainly run 256 DDPM steps per block to establish the strongest performance. In low-NFE regime, it is generally known that deterministic samplers paired with well-tuned time discretization lead to better performance. To investigate the tradeoffs for Sigma , we choose three representative tasks (GSM8K, HumanEval, and MBPP), and vary the diffusion sampling steps from 4 4 to 256 256 . For each sampler-discretization-budget combination, we sweep CFG and ST; we then report, for each combination, the CFG-ST pair that achieves the best average accuracy over the two tasks; see Sec. E.3 for more details. Results. 
As shown in Fig. 14 , DDIM substantially outperforms DDPM at low NFEs while matching DDPM at high NFEs. The uniform- γ \gamma discretization ( Sec. E.2 ) performs the best for a wide range of budgets.
Using the same optimal hyperparameters from the two tasks, we evaluate the 16/32-step performance across all six tasks in Tabs. 1 and 2 ; Tabs. 10 and 11 show performance at other budgets. 
5.2 Distribution Matching Distillation for Few-Step Generation 
Although DDIM has better performance than DDPM at a reduced generation budget, the pretrained models still lose quality at very low NFEs (especially ≤ 16 {\leq}16 steps, see Fig. 7 ). We therefore distill Sigma into a few-step student Sigma - distill using Distribution-Matching Distillation (DMD, Yin et al. (2024b) ; Yin et al. (2024a) ). Notably, the student uses self-conditioning and a purely stochastic sampler that alternates between clean embedding estimation and fresh Gaussian noise injection, which is different from DDIM sampler. Furthermore, our DMD students are trained to match the target distribution without using CFG, which saves inference FLOPs per NFE compared with the guided teacher, and needs no ST rescaling. Results. 
As shown in Figs. 7 , 8 , 10 and 11 , our distillation recipe yields significant acceleration: with only 8 NFEs ( TPF = 4 \text{TPF}=4 ), Sigma - distill matches both NLD ( Fu et al., 2026 ) and our best-performing 256-NFE DDPM sampler on GSM8K and HumanEval at both 3B and 8B scales. For MBPP, which is a full code generation task, syntactic bugs are introduced at low NFE, but Sigma - distill -3B is still within 3% accuracy of the 256-step sampler at 8 NFEs. Interestingly, despite significant acceleration, we observe that Sigma - distill -3B does not lose any diversity compared with its 256-step DDPM teacher (see Sec. F.3 ).
As a result, the distilled model exhibits strong pass@ k k performance: both 4- and 8-NFE Sigma - distill -3B catch up with the 256-NFE DDIM Sigma -3B teacher at k = 256 k=256 (see Fig. 6 ).
Together, this suggests the distilled model is well suited for accelerated RL-based alignment and post-training. 
6 Self-Speculative Decoding 
Sigma trains a block-diffusion stream and a causal AR stream on one backbone, so it can act as its own drafter.
In each round, one denoiser call from pure noise drafts a block of L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 tokens.
The AR stream then scores the block in one causal pass, keeps the longest prefix that matches its own greedy tokens, and appends one token of its own ( Leviathan et al., 2023 ; Fu et al., 2026 ) .
The output is therefore the greedy AR output.
A round of two forward passes commits AL + 1 \mathrm{AL}+1 tokens, where the acceptance length (AL) counts the accepted draft tokens that reach the returned answer. 
At both 3B and 8B scales, we compare NLD to Sigma - distill ( Sec. 5.2 ) to a post-trained model Sigma - distill - spec , which pairs the same distillation objective as Sigma - distill with an acceptance objective (full details in Sec. H.4 ).
We serve with SGLang ( Zheng et al., 2024 ) in BF16 on one NVIDIA GB200, one request at a time, and evaluate on GSM8K, HumanEval, and MBPP.
AL is pooled over the prompts whose outputs neither loop nor reach the length cap. Appendix H details the protocol and gives further results for the undistilled Sigma with trained drafters. Figure 9 : Self-speculative decoding at 3B (top) and 8B (bottom). (a) Throughput relative to the same model’s AR decoding.
(b) Accepted draft tokens per round (AL). Table 3 : Accuracy and AL gain over NLD. Accuracy (%) is that of AR decoding, which speculative decoding reproduces (GSM8K: flexible extract; code: pass@ 1 1 ).
AL gain is relative to NLD of the same size, over the prompts both models keep, with 95% bootstrap intervals.

Figure 9 shows speedup and AL, and Tab. 3 shows accuracy and the AL gain over NLD.
Without any drafter training, Sigma - distill -3B accepts 11–21% more draft tokens per round than NLD-3B, and Sigma - distill -8B accepts 14–23% more than NLD-8B.
We also show in Sec. H.3 that Sigma - distill -3B also accepts 15–22% more tokens than the Sigma -3B, more than what an adapter trained specifically for improving drafting adds to that model. Sigma - distill -8B gains also more than the adapter on GSM8K and MBPP and as much on HumanEval ( Sec. H.3 ).
Further acceptance training (joint distillation and drafter fine-tuning) yields Sigma - distill - spec , which accepts another 5–9% more tokens per round and decodes up to 3.18 × \times (3B) and 4.20 × \times (8B) faster than AR decoding, ahead of NLD on every benchmark and model size.
In all these experiments, the accuracy remains that of the AR mode in each model, since verification returns the greedy AR output. Therefore the speedup comes at no cost relative to AR decoding with the same model. 
7 Supervised Fine-Tuning 
We apply supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to the pretrained Sigma models with the joint AR and block-diffusion losses, restricting supervision to assistant responses. The complete data processing, optimization recipe, and continuous-diffusion-specific controls are given in App. G . Results. 
As shown in Tabs. 4 and 5 , the average performance of Sigma - instruct models is on par with NLD at both 3B and 8B scale, and surpasses other open-source masked dLMs like Dream and LLaDA at the 8B scale. For both 3B checkpoints, we partially re-sweep CFG and ST after SFT and observe that the same optima selected on the corresponding pre-SFT models remain statistically tied for best after SFT ( Tab. 20 ); we therefore retain the pre-SFT settings for the full evaluation. 8 Conclusions 
We introduced Nemotron-Labs-Sigma , a family of large-scale blockwise continuous dLMs obtained by efficient continual pretraining from an AR checkpoint, together with a reverse-diffusion sampling recipe that combines DDPM/DDIM samplers with CFG, ST, and SC.
In diffusion mode, Sigma (3B/8B) outperforms same-size masked dLMs and AR baselines trained under an aligned pipeline, both after pretraining and after SFT, with the most consistent gains on coding.
A sphere-normalized embedding regularization further improves low-NFE sampling and makes model distillation markedly more effective. 
Beyond accuracy, the smoothness of continuous trajectories buys efficiency.
Distribution matching is the strongest of the few-step distillation methods we study: using only 8 NFEs ( TPF = 4 \text{TPF}=4 ), Sigma - distill -3B almost matches both NLD ( Fu et al., 2026 ) and our best-performing 256-NFE DDPM teacher, and it does so without collapsing the teacher’s output distribution, preserving diversity and yielding pass@ k k that keeps improving as k k grows. Because Sigma carries a causal AR stream on the same backbone, the distilled student can also serve as its own one-call drafter: self-speculative decoding returns the greedy AR output at a 3.46 × 3.46\times average speedup over AR decoding at 8B, against 3.04 × 3.04\times for NLD. 
Together, these results establish continuous dLMs as a practical path to efficient language generation.
Looking ahead, the controllable quality–diversity trade-off and the shared diffusion/AR backbone make them particularly attractive for RL-based post-training, which we leave to future work.

Appendix A Related Work 
[Back to Sec. 1 or Sec. 2 in the main paper.] 
This appendix provides a literature review on continuous diffusion language models, the family to which Sigma belongs. Continuous diffusion language models. 
Continuous dLMs lift discrete tokens into a Euclidean space and run a Gaussian (or Riemannian) diffusion there. A central attraction for doing so is that the machinery developed for image diffusion, such as efficient ODE solvers, guidance, and distillation techniques, transfers with little modification. Existing methods differ mainly in where the diffusion process lives: 
• 
Diffusing token embeddings . Diffusion-LM ( Li et al., 2022 ) pioneered this approach with Gaussian diffusion over learned word embeddings and a rounding step back to tokens; CDCD ( Dieleman et al., 2022 ) refined the objective with score interpolation and a categorical cross-entropy loss over softmax logits, and self-conditioned embedding diffusion ( Strudel et al., 2022 ) added self-conditioning on the previous denoising estimate. Plaid ( Gulrajani and Hashimoto, 2023 ) tied these ideas together around a likelihood target by pairing low-dimensional sphere-normalized embeddings with a learned noise schedule and categorical reparameterization; RePlaid ( Yang et al., 2026 ) aligns this recipe with modern DiT architectures and shows it scales competitively with discrete dLMs. Meanwhile, LangFlow ( Chen et al., 2026 ) follows the same learnable-embedding but uses cross-entropy training loss, while Wu et al. (2026) adopts a stochastic-localization formulation. 𝕊 \mathbb{S} -FLM ( Deschenaux and Gulcehre, 2026 ) and Spherical Flows ( Chemseddine et al., 2026 ) likewise use learnable token embeddings but further constrain the diffusion trajectory itself to the embedding hypersphere. A parallel strand fixes the embedding to be one-hot rather than learned: FLM ( Lee et al., 2026 ) and DFM ( Potaptchik et al., 2026 ) run flow matching from a Gaussian source to one-hot vocabulary targets in ℝ V \mathbb{R}^{V} , and further distill the flow into a few-step flow map for fast generation. 
• 
Diffusing an autoencoded latent . Here, a text autoencoder produces a compact continuous latent variable and diffusion is run in that latent space. LD4LG ( Lovelace et al., 2023 ) runs latent diffusion on a frozen encoder–decoder (BART and T5); COSMOS ( Meshchaninov et al., 2025 ) , ELF ( Hu et al., 2026 ) Cola-DLM ( Guo et al., 2026 ) , and AURORA-LM ( Liang et al., 2026 ) scale this axis with different latent designs and training objectives; LDLM ( Meshchaninov et al., 2026 ) learns the encoder and the diffusion prior jointly rather than sequentially. 
• 
Diffusing on the simplex or other statistical manifolds . These methods parameterize a categorical distribution over the vocabulary and diffuse its parameters directly, keeping the noise process on-manifold and avoiding a rounding step. SSD-LM ( Han et al., 2023 ) runs simplex diffusion in a semi-autoregressive block structure, Dirichlet diffusion ( Avdeyev et al., 2023 ) uses a Jacobi diffusion process on the probability simplex, and later variants ( Cheng et al., 2024 ; Davis et al., 2024 ; Jo and Hwang, 2025 ) extend the geometry to Fisher–Rao and Riemannian flow matching. 
• 
Reaching discrete data through dequantization or a Gaussian–discrete duality . Argmax flows ( Hoogeboom et al., 2021 ) lift categorical data into a continuous space via argmax-based variational dequantization, so continuous flows can be trained under a discrete likelihood; analog bits ( Chen et al., 2023 ) , BitLM ( Zhuang et al., 2026 ) , and CoBit ( Batzolis et al., 2026 ) instead map each token to a bitstring and diffuse from there; Duo ( Sahoo et al., 2025 ) establishes a duality between continuous Gaussian and uniform-state discrete diffusion, letting the two families share training and sampling machinery. 
Sigma sits in the token-embedding class, inheriting the learnable embedding and variational diffusion process of Plaid and RePlaid. Scaling continuous dLMs to billions of parameters. 
Recent work pushes continuous dLMs into the billion-parameter regime, but along axes different from ours: for example, Cola-DLM ( Guo et al., 2026 ) ( ∼ \sim 2B, hierarchical latent-prior modeling), AURORA-LM ( Liang et al., 2026 ) (1B, flow matching over an autoencoded latent), and Categorical Flow Maps ( Davis et al., 2026 ) (1.7B, self-distilled for few-step generation). A separate line changes the base representation and scales through an existing LM backbone: BitLM ( Zhuang et al., 2026 ) places a bitwise diffusion head atop a causal LM up to 8B, TESS 2 ( Tae et al., 2025 ) adapts an autoregressive Mistral-7B into a generalist diffusion model with a simplex cross-entropy loss, and DSL-LLaDA ( Yu et al., 2026 ) scales continuous denoising via discrete stochastic localization ( Wu et al., 2026 ) on top of the LLaDA-8B masked-diffusion backbone ( Nie et al., 2025 ) . In contrast, Sigma is, to our knowledge, the first likelihood-trained continuous dLM at 3B/8B, and the first evaluated head-to-head with a matched masked dLM ( Fu et al., 2026 ) on conditional reasoning and code. CFG in dLMs. 
Continuous dLMs realize the guidance condition in two ways. When there is no prompt (e.g., unconditional generation), several works repurpose the self-conditioning signal as the condition: ELF ( Hu et al., 2026 ) and AURORA-LM ( Liang et al., 2026 ) extrapolate between predictions made with and without the previous-step estimate. For conditional generation, DFM ( Potaptchik et al., 2026 ) , AURORA-LM ( Liang et al., 2026 ) , Cola-DLM ( Guo et al., 2026 ) , and BitLM ( Zhuang et al., 2026 ) extrapolate between a prompt-conditioned and an unconditional prediction, as in image diffusion models. A cost for this is that enabling it requires dropping the prompt context during training. FLM ( Lee et al., 2026 ) also leverages autoguidance ( Karras et al., 2024 ) for prediction sharpening.
For discrete dLMs, LLaDA ( Nie et al., 2025 ) popularized an “unsupervised” CFG that runs one forward with full prompt and one with the prompt masked, then linearly extrapolates the two logit vectors before renormalizing; this has since been refined by more general discrete guidance mechanisms ( Schiff et al., 2025 ; Nisonoff et al., 2025 ) .
Our steering ( Sec. 4.2 ) differs on three points: ( i ) the guidance condition is the clean prefix itself, and the unconditional branch simply drops that prefix so the noisy block attends only within itself; ( ii ) the extrapolation acts on the predicted clean embedding (equivalently the score), not on logits; ( iii ) it is a pure inference-time operation that requires no train-time dropout because sequence packing leaves the first block without context. Autoregressive and block video diffusion. 
Our block-causal factorization (i.e., autoregressive across blocks, bidirectional Gaussian denoising within a block over a KV-cacheable clean prefix) closely mirrors recent video generation models that couple causal structure across frames with denoising within them ( Chen et al., 2024 ; Yin et al., 2025 ; Huang et al., 2025 ) . The bidirectional video-diffusion lineage ( Ho et al., 2022 ) was the original target of this conversion, and the autoregressive arm of Cosmos ( Agarwal et al., 2025 ) likewise structures video generation causally across frames. Diffusion Forcing ( Chen et al., 2024 ) trains a causal model with independent per-frame noise levels, unifying next-token prediction with full-sequence diffusion and enabling stable rollouts past the training horizon. CausVid ( Yin et al., 2025 ) and Self Forcing ( Huang et al., 2025 ) convert bidirectional video diffusion into causal, KV-cached autoregressive generators for fast streaming synthesis while curbing error accumulation along the rollout. These works share our central design tension (i.e., coupling causal, autoregressive structure with within-block diffusion) and several of their techniques (per-block noise schedules, rolling KV caches, distribution-matching distillation) transfer naturally to the language setting.

At training, all B B blocks are denoised in a single forward pass: the offset block-causal mask of Fig. 2(b) makes block b b ’s noisy latent attend to the clean prefix 𝐱 ( < b ) {\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})} and bidirectionally within itself, so a per-block time t ( b ) t^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} and a per-block Horvitz–Thompson coin R ( b ) R^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} cost no extra compute. At inference, the conditional and unconditional branches are likewise packed into one forward as in Fig. 5(a) , so the guidance computation costs slightly more wall-clock time per step ( Tab. 19 ) at batch size one. 
The losses are sequence sums, so per-token embedding penalties carry a factor L L . For MS-SUSReg, λ susreg = 1 \lambda_{\text{susreg}}=1 because s MS s_{\mathrm{MS}} already includes the scale weights. SC-selected sequences stop NELBO gradients to the diffusion embedding and schedule while retaining regularizer gradients. MS-SUSReg balances the 25 % 25\% SC fraction across each global batch. 
We further clarify on the learning of the noise schedule, since its two parts are optimized for two different objectives.
The endpoints γ 0 , γ 1 \gamma_{0},\gamma_{1} enter ℒ diff \mathcal{L}_{\text{diff}} through SNR ′ ⁡ ( t ) \operatorname{SNR}^{\prime}(t) and ( α t , σ t ) (\alpha_{t},\sigma_{t}) , so they receive an ordinary NELBO gradient and are trained to tighten the bound while the interior shape γ ~ ψ \tilde{\gamma}_{\psi} does not. As noted in Sec. 2 , the diffusion term is invariant to it in expectation, so ∇ ψ ℒ \nabla_{\psi}\mathcal{L} is zero on average and carries only noise. Following Kingma et al. (2021) , ψ \psi is instead trained to minimize the Monte-Carlo variance of the diffusion estimator, which for a mean-invariant estimator reduces to minimizing its second moment ℒ var \mathcal{L}_{\text{var}} in Alg. 1 . In code, each block’s schedule gradient is scaled by twice its detached mean diffusion loss; reconstruction blocks and SC-selected sequences do not update the shape.
Variant-specific optimizer and learning-rate settings are given in Sec. C.1 . The outer loop of Alg. 2 runs B := ⌈ T gen / S ⌉ B:=\lceil T_{\text{gen}}/S\rceil times, and each window costs N + 1 N+1 denoiser forward passes ( N N diffusion steps plus one decoding readout), for a total of B ⁡ ( N + 1 ) B(N+1) forward passes. The effective NFE per committed token is thus ≈ ( N + 1 ) / S \approx(N+1)/S ,
see Sec. E.7 for a full compute-vs-quality sweep.
Setting w = 1 w=1 disables CFG, τ = 1 \tau=1 disables ST, and never updating 𝐞 sc {\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}} (i.e., leaving it as 𝟎 {\mathbf{0}} ) disables SC; each of these recovers a strictly weaker sampler, as shown in Fig. 15 . 
Appendix C Implementation Details and Additional Results for Pretraining 
[Back to Sec. 3.2 in the main paper.] Architecture. 
Following NLD ( Fu et al., 2026 ) , we adopt the transformer architecture of Ministral ( Liu et al., 2026a ) : 
• 
3B: 26 layers, width 3072, FFN
9216, 32 query and 8 key/value heads (head dimension 128), SwiGLU, RMSNorm, YaRN-scaled rotary
embeddings ( θ = 10 6 \theta=10^{6} ), and untied embeddings. This adds up to 3.0B non-embedding and 3.8B total
parameters. 
• 
8B: 34 layers, width 4096, FFN 14336, same 32 query and 8 key/value heads (head
dimension 128) and same SwiGLU, RMSNorm, YaRN rotary embeddings and untied embeddings. This adds up to 7.4B
non-embedding and 8.5B total parameters. Optimization. 
For Sigma - unreg / Sigma - preg , γ ~ \tilde{\gamma} and 𝐄 {\mathbf{E}} use AdamW with β 1 = 0.9 \beta_{1}=0.9 , β 2 = 0.95 \beta_{2}=0.95 , no weight decay (rows of 𝐄 {\mathbf{E}} unit-norm), and peak learning rate η peak = 1 × 10 − 2 \eta_{\text{peak}}=1\times 10^{-2} ; γ 0 , γ 1 \gamma_{0},\gamma_{1} use the same setting but with weight decay 0.1 0.1 ( Yang et al., 2026 ) . For other parameters, we follow NLD and use AdamW with β 1 = 0.9 \beta_{1}=0.9 , β 2 = 0.95 \beta_{2}=0.95 , weight decay 0.1 0.1 , and η peak = 1 × 10 − 5 \eta_{\text{peak}}=1\times 10^{-5} . For all parameters, we use gradient clipping value 1.0 1.0 . The learning rate schedule is Warmup-Stable-Decay over 150k steps: 1 % 1\% linear warmup to η peak \eta_{\text{peak}} , constant
through step 120k, then exponential decay to 10 % 10\% of η peak \eta_{\text{peak}} . Self-conditioning (SC). 
SC injects the model’s own detached clean-embedding prediction
into the projected noised input stream with a bias-free, zero-initialized linear layer mapping ℝ d e \mathbb{R}^{d_{e}} to the hidden width ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} : a first pass with 𝐞 sc = 𝟎 {\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}}={\mathbf{0}} , then a second pass with d e ​ sg ⁡ ( 𝐞 sc ) \sqrt{d_{e}}\,\operatorname{sg}({\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}}) through that linear layer. Time conditioning. 
For diffusion stream, a single γ \gamma is sampled per block and shared across its tokens. We encode γ \gamma using fixed sinusoidal features with frequencies exp ⁡ ( linspace ⁡ ( − 5 , 5 , 32 ) ) \exp(\mathrm{linspace}(-5,5,32)) , concatenating [ sin , cos ] [\sin,\cos] into 64 dimensions. A bias-free, zero-initialized linear layer projects this encoding to the hidden width, where it is added to the projected noised input. Numerical precision. 
Transformer weights and matrix multiplications, including the linear logit heads, use BF16 ; gradient accumulation and communication use FP32 . The diffusion embedding, noise schedule, gamma endpoints, and input/time/SC projections are stored in FP32 for MS-SUSReg and BF16 for Sigma - unreg / Sigma - preg . Embedding normalization, corruption, input/time/SC projections, and logit postprocessing compute in FP32 ; projected inputs are cast to BF16 for the transformer. Schedule arithmetic and NELBO accumulation use FP64 , following Gulrajani and Hashimoto (2023) ; Yang et al. (2026) . Adam moments and master copies of BF16 weights are FP32 . Optimizer state is sharded across data-parallel ranks, except for MS-SUSReg’s replicated schedule and endpoint states. AR regularization. 
For training Sigma - unreg -3B, we searched over w diff ∈ { 0.3 , 1.0 } w_{\text{diff}}\in\{0.3,1.0\} . Interestingly, when w diff = 1.0 w_{\text{diff}}=1.0 , adding the AR regularization even improved diffusion PPL ( Fig. 3(c) ). However, we found that switching to w diff = 0.3 w_{\text{diff}}=0.3 at 200B tokens (equivalently, 100 ​ k 100\text{k} steps) offers the best convergence for both losses ( Fig. 3(c) ). We thus adopted this schedule ( w diff = 1.0 w_{\text{diff}}=1.0 for the first 100 ​ k 100\text{k} steps and w diff = 0.3 w_{\text{diff}}=0.3 for the last 50 ​ k 50\text{k} steps) for all of our Sigma - unreg -3B/8B and Sigma - preg -3B/8B models (see Sec. C.1 ). For Sigma -3B/8B, we keep w diff = 1.0 w_{\text{diff}}=1.0 all the way through 150 ​ k 150\text{k} steps for simplicity of the training objective. 
C.1 Learning Regularized Embeddings 
[Back to Sec. 4.3 in the main paper.] 
The central goal for our embedding-regularized model variants is to improve the low-NFE performance and distillability by improving the structure of the embeddings.
To this end, we develop the toolbox described in the following: A multiscale variant of Sketched Uniform Spherical Regularization (SUSReg) and a local repulsion loss to combat global and local embedding collapse; a modified Riemannian Adam optimizer step for the embedding table that respects the per-row hyperspherical geometry; a simpler noise schedule parameterization based on Bernstein polynomials that can be trivially initialized to a linear (log-SNR) shape, together with a custom projected optimizer step; and a cosine learning rate schedule for the embeddings to aid their stable learning. Sketched Uniform Spherical Regularization (SUSReg). 
To avoid the V = 131072 V=131072 embedding vectors in d e = 16 d_{e}=16 dimensional unit sphere being too tightly clustered for accurate decoding (as observed in their low-rank structure in Yang et al. (2026) ), we propose to add an optional regularization term based on SUSReg ( Nicollier et al., 2026 ) to the embeddings, which regularizes their distribution p 𝐄 := 1 V ​ ∑ v = 1 V δ 𝐞 v p_{\mathbf{E}}:=\frac{1}{V}\sum_{v=1}^{V}\delta_{{\mathbf{e}}_{v}} toward Unif ⁡ ( 𝕊 d e − 1 ) \operatorname{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d_{e}-1}) , the uniform distribution on the unit sphere in ℝ d e \mathbb{R}^{d_{e}} . 
For M M directions 𝐚 m ∼ Unif ⁡ ( 𝕊 d e − 1 ) \mathbf{a}_{m}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d_{e}-1}) , we compare the empirical characteristic function of each one-dimensional projection, 
ϕ 𝐚 m ​ ( t ) = 1 V ​ ∑ v e i ​ t ​ ⟨ 𝐚 m , 𝐞 v ⟩ , \displaystyle\phi_{\mathbf{a}_{m}}(t)=\frac{1}{V}\sum_{v}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}t\langle\mathbf{a}_{m},{\mathbf{e}}_{v}\rangle}, 
to the target characteristic function of a one-dimensional projection of Unif ⁡ ( 𝕊 d e − 1 ) \operatorname{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d_{e}-1}) , whose marginal density is known in closed form: 
ϕ d e ​ ( t ) = ∫ − 1 1 cos ⁡ ( t ​ x ) ​ ρ d e ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x , ρ d e ​ ( x ) = Γ ⁡ ( d e 2 ) π ​ Γ ​ ( d e − 1 2 ) ​ ( 1 − x 2 ) d e − 3 2 ​ 𝟏 | x | ≤ 1 , \displaystyle\phi_{d_{e}}(t)=\int_{-1}^{1}\cos(tx)\rho_{d_{e}}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x,\quad\rho_{d_{e}}(x)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{d_{e}}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(\frac{d_{e}-1}{2})}(1-x^{2})^{\frac{d_{e}-3}{2}}{\mathbf{1}}_{|x|\leq 1}, 
under an Epps–Pulley Gaussian-weighted L 2 L^{2} metric: 
s ( 𝐄 ) := V M ∑ m = 1 M ∫ − t max t max | ϕ 𝐚 m ( t ) − ϕ d e ( t ) | 2 e − t 2 / 2 d t . \displaystyle s({\mathbf{E}}):=\frac{V}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\int_{-t_{\max}}^{t_{\max}}\bigl|\phi_{\mathbf{a}_{m}}(t)-\phi_{d_{e}}(t)\bigr|^{2}{\mathrm{e}}^{-t^{2}/2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t. (7) 
The multiplier V V compensates for the 1 / V 1/V scaling of the expected squared characteristic-function discrepancy under i.i.d. uniform embeddings.
We add λ susreg ​ s ​ ( 𝐄 ) \lambda_{\text{susreg}}s({\mathbf{E}}) per token on the diffusion arm, contributing w diff ​ λ susreg ​ s ​ ( 𝐄 ) w_{\mathrm{diff}}\lambda_{\text{susreg}}s({\mathbf{E}}) to the joint objective ( Alg. 1 ). Plain SUSReg-3B/8B uses λ susreg = 0.005 \lambda_{\text{susreg}}=0.005 , M = 512 M=512 freshly sampled directions, and P = 17 P=17 trapezoidal points over [ − 5 , 5 ] [-5,5] . The target characteristic function is cached using 512-point numerical quadrature. The O ⁡ ( V ​ M ​ ( d e + P ) ) O(VM(d_{e}+P)) cost is negligible relative to the transformer. Figure 10 : Density plot of a random 𝕊 15 → 𝕊 2 \mathbb{S}^{15}\to\mathbb{S}^{2} Mollweide projection of all learned embeddings living on 𝕊 15 \mathbb{S}^{15} .
The baseline collapses to a small subregion of the 16D space, while SUSReg distributes density more evently, but still contains many concentrated clusters. MS-SUSReg achieves a comparatively much more uniform distribution. Note that while the MS-SUSReg cloud looks uniform in density, it is far from simple random initialization, see the conceptual hierarchical clustering shown in Fig. 11 .
The PCA effective dimensions of three embedding matrices at 150k steps are 3.4008 , 15.9593 , 15.9986 3.4008,15.9593,15.9986 , respectively. Figure 11 : Dendrogram on a set of single-token example words, constructed by bottom-up average-linkage clustering of the selected unit-normalized embedding rows in the 3B model variants (Unregularized baseline, SUSReg, MS-SUSReg), using Euclidean distances, with the cluster merge distance shown on the y-axis. Each shown word represents the lowercase token form prefixed by a single space, e.g., " dog" . Top: The unregularized embedding table exhibits very low clustering distances, consistent with subspace collapse, and also contains some counterintuitive clusterings that mix categories. Middle: The SUSReg run greatly improves upon both issues, but most merges still occur at low distances and some unusual groupings (train, plum) remain. Bottom: The MS-SUSReg run exhibits the cleanest clustering in this example, corresponding well to natural categories, and also shows larger merge distances already at lower levels of the hierarchy. Multiscale-SUSReg (MS-SUSReg). 
The SUSReg regularizer 7 contains the Gaussian kernel e − t 2 / 2 {\mathrm{e}}^{-t^{2}/2} , which corresponds to a specific low-pass weighting of the frequencies in the compared characteristic functions. This means the loss is more sensitive to larger-scale structural mismatches (global uniformity) than to the finer scales (local clustering). We generalize this by introducing a kernel width parameter β > 0 \beta>0 : 
s β ​ ( 𝐄 ) \displaystyle s_{\beta}({\mathbf{E}}) : = V M ∑ m = 1 M ∫ − β ​ t max β ​ t max 1 β | ϕ 𝐚 m ( t ) − ϕ d e ( t ) | 2 e − t 2 / ( 2 β 2 ) d t \displaystyle:=\frac{V}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\int_{-\beta t_{\max}}^{\beta t_{\max}}\frac{1}{\beta}\bigl|\phi_{\mathbf{a}_{m}}(t)-\phi_{d_{e}}(t)\bigr|^{2}{\mathrm{e}}^{-t^{2}/(2\beta^{2})}\,{\mathrm{d}}t (8) 
= V M ∑ m = 1 M ∫ − t max t max | ϕ 𝐚 m ( β u ) − ϕ d e ( β u ) | 2 e − u 2 / 2 d u \displaystyle=\frac{V}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\int_{-t_{\max}}^{t_{\max}}\bigl|\phi_{\mathbf{a}_{m}}(\beta u)-\phi_{d_{e}}(\beta u)\bigr|^{2}{\mathrm{e}}^{-u^{2}/2}\,{\mathrm{d}}u (9) 
where the second form is from a simple change of variables with u = t / β u=t/\beta . The original SUSReg loss 7 corresponds to β = 1 \beta=1 . To regularize both the global and the local structure, we can then sum K K loss terms like 8 with different weights w k w_{k} and kernel widths β k \beta_{k} : 
s MS ( 𝐄 ) := V M ∑ m = 1 M ∑ k = 1 K w k ∫ − t max t max | ϕ 𝐚 m ( β k t ) − ϕ d e ( β k t ) | 2 e − t 2 / 2 d t \displaystyle s_{\mathrm{MS}}({\mathbf{E}}):=\frac{V}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{k}\int_{-t_{\max}}^{t_{\max}}\bigl|\phi_{\mathbf{a}_{m}}(\beta_{k}t)-\phi_{d_{e}}(\beta_{k}t)\bigr|^{2}{\mathrm{e}}^{-t^{2}/2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t (10) 
which we call the Multiscale-SUSReg (MS-SUSReg) loss. Its scale weights w k w_{k} are absolute coefficients: the per-token objective contains w diff ​ s MS ​ ( 𝐄 ) w_{\mathrm{diff}}s_{\mathrm{MS}}({\mathbf{E}}) , without an additional SUSReg multiplier.
Each bandwidth uses P k = 1 + ⌈ β k ​ ( P − 1 ) ⌉ P_{k}=1+\lceil\beta_{k}(P-1)\rceil quadrature points to preserve frequency resolution: 17 and 257 points for β = 1 \beta=1 and 16 16 . The scales share M = 512 M=512 random directions and embedding projections, use t max = 5 t_{\max}=5 , and are active from step zero. Cached targets give a total cost of O ⁡ ( V ​ M ​ ( d e + ∑ k P k ) ) O(VM(d_{e}+\sum_{k}P_{k})) . Local repulsion regularization. 
MS-SUSReg encourages distributional uniformity through sampled projections, but does not explicitly enforce separation between individual embedding pairs. Nearly coincident embeddings can therefore remain difficult to distinguish under diffusion noise. To address this directly, we add a local separation penalty related to maximum hyperspherical separation ( Liu et al., 2021 ) . Namely, we add a per-token weighted loss term w diff ​ λ rep ​ s rep ​ ( 𝐄 ) w_{\mathrm{diff}}\lambda_{\mathrm{rep}}s_{\mathrm{rep}}({\mathbf{E}}) , where 
s rep ​ ( 𝐄 ) := 1 2 ​ V ​ ∑ u < v max ⁡ { 0 , r − ‖ 𝐞 u − 𝐞 v ‖ 2 } 2 \displaystyle s_{\mathrm{rep}}({\mathbf{E}}):=\frac{1}{2V}\sum_{u<v}\max\{0,r-\|{\mathbf{e}}_{u}-{\mathbf{e}}_{v}\|_{2}\}^{2} (11) 
penalizes pairwise chord distances below an exclusion radius r r . We set r = max ⁡ { 2 ​ ( 1 − c ) , 2 ​ Φ − 1 ​ ( 1 − p ) ​ e γ 0 / 2 } r=\max\{\sqrt{2(1-c)},2\Phi^{-1}(1-p){\mathrm{e}}^{\gamma_{0}/2}\} , where Φ \Phi is the standard normal CDF, and c = 0.9995 c=0.9995 , p = 0.01 p=0.01 , and λ rep = 20 \lambda_{\mathrm{rep}}=20 .
Unlike maximum hyperspherical separation, which maximizes the smallest pairwise distance, our penalty acts on all pairs within r r .
The adaptive term depending on γ 0 \gamma_{0} defines a target separation corresponding to a per-competitor confusion probability of p p under the diffusion forward marginal distribution at the minimum noise level γ 0 \gamma_{0} . We disable gradients through r r here so this loss only affects the embeddings and not γ 0 \gamma_{0} .
We evaluate all unordered pairs in tiles across data-parallel workers without storing the full pairwise matrix. Both embedding regularizers act from step zero, including on SC-selected sequences, and are excluded from reported NELBO/perplexity. Riemannian Adam step. 
Since the effective embeddings have unit norm, their geometry depends only on their directions. The naive embedding representation, which normalizes each unconstrained row on the fly when used, creates a per-row dependence of the effective learning rate on arbitrary underlying pre-normalization row norms. We find that these row norms grow over training, resulting in an uncontrolled LR decay on the embedding rows. With the following Riemannian Adam step, we optimize the embedding rows directly on the sphere to make every update respect the on-sphere constraint and avoid this uncontrolled influence on the embedding LR. For each row 𝐞 v {\mathbf{e}}_{v} , we project its total training-loss gradient 𝐠 v \mathbf{g}_{v} onto the tangent space at 𝐞 v {\mathbf{e}}_{v} as 𝐠 v ⟂ = 𝐠 v − ⟨ 𝐠 v , 𝐞 v ⟩ ​ 𝐞 v \mathbf{g}_{v}^{\perp}=\mathbf{g}_{v}-\langle\mathbf{g}_{v},{\mathbf{e}}_{v}\rangle{\mathbf{e}}_{v} . Similar to Becigneul and Ganea (2019) , we adapt the step size separately for each embedding vector. We apply a bias-corrected on-manifold Adam variant using a tangent first moment and one scalar second moment per row, estimated from ‖ 𝐠 v ⟂ ‖ 2 2 / d e \|\mathbf{g}_{v}^{\perp}\|_{2}^{2}/d_{e} . This scalar second moment rescales all tangent directions equally. After the update, we project the row back onto the sphere by normalization and parallel-transport the first moment along the shortest spherical geodesic to the new tangent space, preserving momentum as the embedding direction changes. Projected noise-schedule updates. 
Our main regularized model ( Sigma -3B/8B) uses a degree-32 Bernstein polynomial for the noise schedule shape with increments ψ i ≥ 10 − 3 / 32 \psi_{i}\geq 10^{-3}/32 , ∑ i ψ i = 1 \sum_{i}\psi_{i}=1 : 
γ ~ ψ ​ ( t ) \displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{\psi}(t) = ∑ j = 0 32 c j ​ ( 32 j ) ​ t j ​ ( 1 − t ) 32 − j , c 0 = 0 , c j = ∑ i = 1 j ψ i . \displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{32}c_{j}\binom{32}{j}t^{j}(1-t)^{32-j},\qquad c_{0}=0,\quad c_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\psi_{i}. 
Initializing ψ i = 1 / 32 \psi_{i}=1/32 gives a linear schedule. Adam updates on the variance objective are projected onto this simplex; endpoint AdamW updates use weight decay 0.1 0.1 and enforce γ 1 − γ 0 ≥ 10 − 3 \gamma_{1}-\gamma_{0}\geq 10^{-3} . The embedding and shape have no weight decay. All three groups use Adam coefficients ( β 1 , β 2 ) = ( 0.9 , 0.95 ) (\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0.9,0.95) and ϵ = 10 − 8 \epsilon=10^{-8} . Training details for the regularized models. 
For the plain SUSReg ablation models (Sigma -preg-3B/8B): 3 3 3 “ preg ” stands for plain SUSReg. we add the described SUSReg loss in 7 and freeze the embeddings and schedule parameters after 100k steps, but otherwise follow the original model’s training recipe including the change to w diff = 0.3 w_{\mathrm{diff}}=0.3 at 100k steps. For MS-SUSReg (Sigma-3B/8B): we use ( β 1 , w 1 ) = ( 1 , 10 − 2 ) (\beta_{1},w_{1})=(1,10^{-2}) , ( β 2 , w 2 ) = ( 16 , 10 − 4 ) (\beta_{2},w_{2})=(16,10^{-4}) , and repulsion weight λ rep = 20 \lambda_{\mathrm{rep}}=20 . The embedding uses Riemannian Adam; the schedule uses the projected updates above. Their learning rates warm up to 3 × 10 − 3 3\times 10^{-3} over 1.5k steps, decay by cosine to 10 − 4 10^{-4} at 30k, remain constant through 120k, then follow WSD decay to 10 − 5 10^{-5} at 150k. Embeddings and schedules remain trainable throughout, with w diff = 1 w_{\mathrm{diff}}=1 . Global gradient-norm clipping is 3 for 3B and 1 for 8B. Full pretrained results for all three variants. 
Table 6 reports the six generative reasoning tasks of Tabs. 1 and 2 for all three pretrained variants at both scales. In diffusion mode, plain SUSReg is roughly neutral against Sigma - unreg at DDPM-256 but gains 1.40 1.40 (3B) and 0.79 0.79 (8B) average points at DDIM-32, consistent with our hypothesis that uniform embeddings mainly buy robustness to coarse discretization; MS-SUSReg improves on both at every operating point and scale. C.2 Ablation Study: MDLM Initialization for Pretraining 
[Back to Sec. 3.2 in the main paper.] 
All Sigma models featured in our main results are warm-started from an AR checkpoint (“AR initialization” in Tab. 1 ) and then trained for 300 300 B tokens under the joint diffusion + AR objective ( Sec. 3.2.2 ). We ablate that choice against a masked diffusion language model (MDLM) warm start: identical 300 300 B-token recipe, run instead from the released Nemotron-Labs-Diffusion-3B checkpoint of Fu et al. (2026) , which is our main baseline in Tab. 1 . All other knobs are held fixed. MDLM initialization has an initial advantage that dies off during learning rate decay. Evaluation setup.
