We evaluate on ARC-e/c ( Clark et al., 2018 ) , HellaSwag ( Zellers et al., 2019 ) , PIQA ( Bisk et al., 2020 ) , WinoGrande ( Sakaguchi et al., 2020 ) , and MMLU ( Hendrycks et al., 2021 ) .
These benchmarks score a candidate response 𝐫 {\mathbf{r}} against a prompt 𝐩 {\mathbf{p}} by comparing log ⁡ p θ ​ ( 𝐫 ∣ 𝐩 ) \log p_{\theta}({\mathbf{r}}\mid{\mathbf{p}}) across candidates.
Per Open LLM Leaderboard convention, we report the plain likelihood on WinoGrande and MMLU, and the likelihood normalized by character length on the other four tasks which are length-varying. Scoring our continuous method. 
Sigma does not give likelihood in closed form, but a surrogate based on the NELBO: taking the prompt as the clean prefix and the candidate as the block to be denoised, and then applying 4 verbatim with 𝐩 {\mathbf{p}} as 𝐱 ( < b ) {\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})} and 𝐫 {\mathbf{r}} as 𝐱 ( b ) {\mathbf{x}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})} : 
− log ⁡ p θ ​ ( 𝐫 ∣ 𝐩 ) ≤ ℒ NELBO ​ ( 𝐫 , 𝐩 ) \displaystyle-\log p_{\theta}({\mathbf{r}}\mid{\mathbf{p}})\leq\mathcal{L}_{\text{NELBO}}({\mathbf{r}};{\mathbf{p}}) = KL ( q ( 𝐳 1 ∣ 𝐫 ) ∥ p ( 𝐳 1 ) ) + 𝔼 q ⁡ ( 𝐳 0 ∣ 𝐫 ) [ ∑ l = 1 | 𝐫 | − log ⟨ 𝐱 θ l ( 𝐳 0 , 0 ; 𝐩 ) , 𝐫 l ⟩ ] \displaystyle=\operatorname{KL}(q({\mathbf{z}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{r}})\,\|\,p({\mathbf{z}}_{1}))+\E_{q({\mathbf{z}}_{0}\mid{\mathbf{r}})}\big[{\textstyle\sum}_{l=1}^{|{\mathbf{r}}|}-\log\langle{\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}^{l}({\mathbf{z}}_{0},0;{\mathbf{p}}),{\mathbf{r}}^{l}\rangle\big] 
− 1 2 ​ 𝔼 t ∼ Unif ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] ​ 𝔼 q ⁡ ( 𝐳 t ∣ 𝐫 ) ​ [ SNR ′ ⁡ ( t ) ​ ‖ 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t , t , 𝐩 ) − 𝐞 ‖ 2 ] . \displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\ \E_{t\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]}\E_{q({\mathbf{z}}_{t}\mid{\mathbf{r}})}[\operatorname{SNR}^{\prime}(t)\|{\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t},t;{\mathbf{p}})-{\mathbf{e}}\|^{2}]. (12) 
In practice, we evaluate 12 with 128 128 stratified samples t k = k + u 128 t_{k}=\frac{k+u}{128} , k = 0 , … , 127 k=0,\dots,127 , u ∼ Unif ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] u\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1] and 8 8 reconstruction samples. Scoring the masked baseline via count-based NELBO. 
We first give a brief introduction of masked diffusion models ( Sahoo et al., 2024 ; Shi et al., 2024 ; Ou et al., 2025 ) . There, the latent 𝐳 t ∈ 𝒱 L {\mathbf{z}}_{t}\in\mathcal{V}^{L} is a partially masked token sequence: each position is independently kept clean with probability 1 − t 1-t or replaced by the mask token 𝐦 {\mathbf{m}} with probability t t . The denoiser 𝐱 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ) ∈ ( Δ V ) L ⊂ ℝ L × V {\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t})\in(\Delta^{V})^{L}\subset\mathbb{R}^{L\times V} returns a categorical distribution over clean tokens at each position; one can prove that it needs not to take time argument as t t carries no information about 𝐱 {\mathbf{x}} beyond what 𝐳 t {\mathbf{z}}_{t} already reveals ( Ou et al., 2025 ) . The resulting NELBO is a 1 t \frac{1}{t} -weighted cross entropy over the masked positions only. 
Let 𝐩 {\mathbf{p}} be the prompt and 𝐫 {\mathbf{r}} be a candidate response. We generate the masked states by the mask set , instead of randomly masking each position with probability t t : for M ⊆ { 1 , … , L 𝐫 } M\subseteq\{1,\dots,L_{\mathbf{r}}\} , where L 𝐫 := | 𝐫 | L_{\mathbf{r}}:=|{\mathbf{r}}| , we obtain 𝐳 M {\mathbf{z}}_{M} from masking indices in M M of 𝐫 {\mathbf{r}} , and denote C ( M ) := ∑ l ∈ M − log ⟨ 𝐱 θ l ( 𝐳 M ; 𝐩 ) , 𝐫 l ⟩ C(M):=\sum_{l\in M}-\log\langle{\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}^{l}({\mathbf{z}}_{M};{\mathbf{p}}),\,{\mathbf{r}}^{l}\rangle their cross entropy. Conditioning that bound on 𝐩 {\mathbf{p}} and integrating t t out yields an average over
the number of masked tokens ( Ou et al., 2025 ; Nie et al., 2025 ) : 
− log ⁡ p θ ​ ( 𝐫 ∣ 𝐩 ) ≤ ℒ MDLM ​ ( 𝐫 , 𝐩 ) \displaystyle-\log p_{\theta}({\mathbf{r}}\mid{\mathbf{p}})\;\leq\;\mathcal{L}_{\text{MDLM}}({\mathbf{r}};{\mathbf{p}}) = 𝔼 t ∼ Unif ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] ⁡ [ 1 t ​ 𝔼 M ∼ Ber ​ ( t ) ⊗ L 𝐫 ⁡ C ⁡ ( M ) ] \displaystyle\;=\;\E_{t\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\,\E_{M\sim\mathrm{Ber}(t)^{\otimes L_{\mathbf{r}}}}C(M)\Big] (13) 
= 𝔼 n ∼ Unif ⁡ { 1 , … , L 𝐫 } ⁡ [ L 𝐫 n ​ 𝔼 M ∼ Unif ⁡ ( [ L 𝐫 ] n ) ⁡ C ⁡ ( M ) ] , \displaystyle\;=\;\E_{n\sim\operatorname{Unif}\{1,\ldots,L_{\mathbf{r}}\}}\Big[\frac{L_{\mathbf{r}}}{n}\,\E_{M\sim\operatorname{Unif}\binom{[L_{\mathbf{r}}]}{n}}C(M)\Big], (14) 
where ( [ L 𝐫 ] n ) \binom{[L_{\mathbf{r}}]}{n} means the set of all n n -element subsets of [ L 𝐫 ] := { 1 , 2 , … , L 𝐫 } [L_{\mathbf{r}}]:=\{1,2,...,L_{\mathbf{r}}\} . 14 is a Rao–Blackwellized low-variance estimator of 13 , manifested in the following three major differences: (i) The 1 t \frac{1}{t} weight becomes L 𝐫 n ≤ L 𝐫 \frac{L_{\mathbf{r}}}{n}\leq L_{\mathbf{r}} , so rare small- t t draws that leave one or two masked tokens would not cause the variance of the estimator to blow up. (ii) Bernoulli masking leaves 𝐫 {\mathbf{r}} wholly unmasked with
probability ( 1 − t ) L 𝐫 (1-t)^{L_{\mathbf{r}}} , which may be common for short responses and wastes a forward pass, while the n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 constraint avoids this. (iii) As n n is finite, we can similarly use stratified sampling as the t t -grid above, spreading counts evenly
over { 1 , … , L 𝐫 } \{1,\ldots,L_{\mathbf{r}}\} from one uniform offset, so every batch covers all masking rates. 
In practice, we use 14 with 128 128 low-discrepancy draws n k n_{k} and standard random sampling for M k M_{k} for Nemotron-Labs-Diffusion ( Fu et al., 2026 ) in Tabs. 8 and 9 . On MMLU candidates are
single tokens, so L 𝐫 = n = 1 L_{\mathbf{r}}=n=1 and 14 is exact after one forward pass, while 13 would still be averaging 1 t \frac{1}{t} . We found 14 to work better than 13 in early experiments. Length generalization. 
Diffusion training denoises blocks of exactly L ′ L^{\prime} tokens from packed sequences of length L L , whereas candidates are
typically short and occasionally longer than L ′ L^{\prime} . For each ( 𝐩 , 𝐫 ) ({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{r}}) pair, we apply causal attention over 𝐩 {\mathbf{p}} and bidirectional attention over 𝐫 {\mathbf{r}} , assuming that the bidirectional attention can generalize to response length other than L ′ L^{\prime} . The attention map follows the conditional branch in Fig. 5(a) .

E.1 Sampler Updates 
Let 1 = t 0 > ⋯ > t N = 0 1=t_{0}>\cdots>t_{N}=0 , with α i = α t i \alpha_{i}=\alpha_{t_{i}} , σ i = σ t i \sigma_{i}=\sigma_{t_{i}} , ρ i = σ i / α i \rho_{i}=\sigma_{i}/\alpha_{i} , and λ i = log ( α i / σ i ) = − γ ( t i ) / 2 \lambda_{i}=\log(\alpha_{i}/\sigma_{i})=-\gamma(t_{i})/2 .
At step i i , the clean prediction 𝐞 ^ i = 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 i , t i , 𝐜 ) \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}={\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{i},t_{i};{\mathbf{c}}) implies ϵ ^ i = ( 𝐳 i − α i ​ 𝐞 ^ i ) / σ i \hat{\bm{\epsilon}}_{i}=({\mathbf{z}}_{i}-\alpha_{i}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i})/\sigma_{i} .
Predictions incorporate CFG and ST as in CFG and ST . DDPM. 
The ancestral VDM update ( Kingma et al., 2021 , App. A.4) uses the Gaussian posterior with 𝐞 ^ i \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i} replacing the clean
embedding: 
𝐳 i + 1 = ( 1 − c i ) ​ α i + 1 α i ​ 𝐳 i + c i ​ α i + 1 ​ 𝐞 ^ i + σ i + 1 ​ c i ​ ϵ , c i = 1 − e γ ⁡ ( t i + 1 ) − γ ⁡ ( t i ) , ϵ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 ) . {\mathbf{z}}_{i+1}=(1-c_{i})\frac{\alpha_{i+1}}{\alpha_{i}}{\mathbf{z}}_{i}+c_{i}\alpha_{i+1}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}+\sigma_{i+1}\sqrt{c_{i}}\,{\bm{\epsilon}},\quad c_{i}=1-{\mathrm{e}}^{\gamma(t_{i+1})-\gamma(t_{i})},\quad{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{I}}). (15) DDIM. 
DDIM ( Song et al., 2021a ) deterministically combines the
current clean and noise predictions: 
𝐳 i + 1 DDIM = α i + 1 ​ 𝐞 ^ i + σ i + 1 ​ ϵ ^ i . {\mathbf{z}}_{i+1}^{\mathrm{DDIM}}=\alpha_{i+1}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}+\sigma_{i+1}\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}_{i}. (16) 
It is the first-order update obtained by freezing either prediction
in the corresponding integrating-factor formulation of the PFODE. 
E.2 Ablation Study: Time Discretization 
[Back to Sec. 5.1 in the main paper.] Figure 13 : Left: the learned noise schedule γ ⁡ ( t ) \gamma(t) for Sigma - unreg -3B. Right: Different time discretization schemes for 16-step generation. Note that an extra decoding step is needed at t = 0 t=0 . Figure 14 : DDIM substantially outperforms DDPM at low NFEs for Sigma - unreg -3B, while matching DDPM at high NFEs. 
We mainly consider the following discretization schemes. Let N N be the total number of steps and assume the i i -th step ( i = 1 , … , N i=1,...,N ) goes from t i − 1 t_{i-1} to t i < t i − 1 t_{i}<t_{i-1} . 
• 
Learned: arithmetic progression in time: t i = 1 − i N t_{i}=1-\frac{i}{N} . 
• 
Uniform- γ \gamma : arithmetic progression in γ \gamma , the minus log-SNR: γ ⁡ ( t i ) = γ max + i N ​ ( γ min − γ max ) \gamma(t_{i})=\gamma_{\max}+\frac{i}{N}(\gamma_{\min}-\gamma_{\max}) . 
• 
EDM ρ \rho ( Karras et al., 2022 ) : first convert the VP schedule to VE by defining σ ~ t := σ t α t \tilde{\sigma}_{t}:=\frac{\sigma_{t}}{\alpha_{t}} as the essential standard deviation at time t t . The grid points are chosen so that σ ~ t i 1 / ρ \tilde{\sigma}_{t_{i}}^{1/\rho} is an arithmetic progression, i.e., σ ~ t i = ( σ ~ max 1 / ρ + i N ​ ( σ ~ min 1 / ρ − σ ~ max 1 / ρ ) ) ρ \tilde{\sigma}_{t_{i}}=\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\max}^{1/\rho}+\frac{i}{N}(\tilde{\sigma}_{\min}^{1/\rho}-\tilde{\sigma}_{\max}^{1/\rho})\right)^{\rho} .
Note that as ρ → ∞ \rho\to\infty , this converges to the uniform- γ \gamma scheme. 
We visualize the three schemes in Fig. 13 . Empirically, we find that for high NFE sampling, both the uniform- γ \gamma and EDM ρ = 7 \rho=7 schemes perform similarly, while uniform- γ \gamma generally performs the best for low NFE sampling. See Fig. 14 for the few-step reasoning accuracy of DDPM and DDIM over GSM8K and HumanEval. 
E.3 Reasoning and Coding Benchmark Setup 
[Back to Secs. 4 and 5 in the main paper.] 
We describe the harness, scoring pipeline, and sampler that produce Tabs. 1 , 2 , 10 and 11 . The scoring pipeline is consistent for all base models and generation modes. Benchmarks and few-shot budgets. 
We use six tasks from lm-evaluation-harness following Fu et al. (2026) :
GSM8K (8-shot, 1319 questions) ( Cobbe et al., 2021 ) , Minerva Math (4-shot, 5000 questions 4 4 4 Number of questions in each sub-domain: algebra 1187, counting/prob 474, geometry 479, intermediate algebra 903, number theory 540, prealgebra 871, precalc 546. ) ( Lewkowycz et al., 2022 ) , HumanEval and HumanEval+ (0-shot, 164 questions) ( Chen et al., 2021 ) , and MBPP and MBPP+ (3-shot, 500 and 378 questions, respectively) ( Austin et al., 2021 ) .
The per-task generation budget is 256 tokens for GSM8K and 512 tokens for the other five tasks. Scoring. 
GSM8K is scored by exact-match on the numerical answer, extracted from the model’s response by a permissive regex that keeps the last number appearing in the completion (stripping separators and trailing punctuation).
Minerva Math is scored by symbolic equivalence after canonicalizing boxed and inline expressions.
HumanEval and HumanEval+ report post-processed pass@ 1 1 : the completion is stripped out of the Markdown code fence, an AST-based longest-valid-code extraction is applied, and the resulting program is executed against the task test cases in the container.
MBPP and MBPP+ report the harness pass@ 1 1 directly without this post-processing step, matching the convention of prior work. Decoding configuration. 
All Sigma diffusion-mode runs use block size L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 , BF16 model weights at inference time, and operation of SC follows Fig. 5(b) . The DDPM-256 row uses the ancestral sampler of 15 on the EDM ρ = 7 \rho=7 time discretization ( Sec. E.2 ); the DDIM-32 row uses the deterministic sampler of 16 on the uniform- γ \gamma time discretization. Seed error bars. 
Each Sigma diffusion row aggregates 10 10 independent sampling lanes as follows. For question i i , we independently draw 10 10 responses under the same model with 10 10 different seeds. Let s i , j s_{i,j} be the score of the j j -th response to question i i . We first average over questions to obtain 10 10 per-lane scores s j = mean i ⁡ ( s i , j ) s_{j}=\operatorname{mean}_{i}(s_{i,j}) for j = 1 , … , 10 j=1,\dots,10 , and then obtain s ¯ = mean j ⁡ ( s j ) \bar{s}=\operatorname{mean}_{j}(s_{j}) together with the across-lane standard deviation σ ^ = std j ⁡ ( s j ) \hat{\sigma}=\operatorname{std}_{j}(s_{j}) . The half-widths in Tab. 1 are 2 10 ​ σ ^ \frac{2}{\sqrt{10}}\hat{\sigma} , an approximate 95 % 95\% confidence interval for the mean. Baseline decoders. 
The AR-mode for NLD and Sigma row uses greedy argmax at each step; NLD’s diffusion-mode row uses the confidence-threshold greedy sampler of Fu et al. (2026) (block size L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 ; argmax \argmax readout at every position whose top-1 probability exceeds a threshold; we report the better of thresholds 0.8 0.8 and 0.9 0.9 per task). For LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B we quote each model’s best diffusion-mode number without the confidence-threshold rule: at each denoising step the single position with the highest top-1 confidence is unmasked.
Both rules are instances of confidence-based decoding ( Chang et al., 2022 ) , and both inherit the absorbing property of masked diffusion: once a position is unmasked, its token is frozen for the rest of the trajectory.

[Back to Sec. 4.2 or Sec. 4.4 in the main paper.] 
In Figs. 15 and 16 , we present the full tables for CFG and ST sweep for our Sigma - unreg -3B models trained with and without AR regularization, respectively. E.5 Ablation Study: Auto-Guidance with Self-Conditioning 
[Back to Sec. 4.2 in the main paper.] 
Standard CFG ( Ho and Salimans, 2022 ) , also following the idea of auto-guidance ( Karras et al., 2024 ) , extrapolates a conditional prediction away from a “bad” prediction to sharpen the model’s prediction. Self-conditioning introduces a second source of conditioning, as is used in existing continuous dLMs (e.g., Lee et al. (2026) ; Potaptchik et al. (2026) ; Hu et al. (2026) ; see App. A for detailed discussion).
We define the following notations in this subsection: 
𝐞 θ + := 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 , 𝐞 sc ) , 𝐞 θ 𝐜 , 𝟎 := 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , 𝐜 , 𝟎 ) , 𝐞 θ ∅ , 𝟎 := 𝐞 θ ​ ( 𝐳 t ( b ) , t , ∅ , 𝟎 ) , \displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{\theta}:={\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\text{sc}}),\qquad{\mathbf{e}}^{{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta}:={\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{0}}),\qquad{\mathbf{e}}^{\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta}:={\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}({\mathbf{z}}_{t}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}b})},t;\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}), 
where the good prediction 𝐞 θ + {\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{\theta} is the conditional prediction that uses both the clean context and SC; for either bad prediction 𝐞 θ − ∈ { 𝐞 θ 𝐜 , 𝟎 , 𝐞 θ ∅ , 𝟎 } {\mathbf{e}}^{-}_{\theta}\in\{{\mathbf{e}}^{{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta},{\mathbf{e}}^{\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta}\} , the guided mean embedding prediction is 
𝐞 θ w = 𝐞 θ + + ( w − 1 ) ​ ( 𝐞 θ + − 𝐞 θ − ) . \displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}^{w}_{\theta}={\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{\theta}+(w-1)({\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{\theta}-{\mathbf{e}}^{-}_{\theta}). 
The context-preserving baseline keeps the prompt and previously generated blocks in both branches and sets 𝐞 θ − ← 𝐞 θ 𝐜 , 𝟎 {\mathbf{e}}^{-}_{\theta}\leftarrow{\mathbf{e}}^{{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta} ; it therefore extrapolates only away from the prediction made without SC.
Our proposed method in Sec. 4.2 instead uses the fully unconditional branch 𝐞 θ − ← 𝐞 θ ∅ , 𝟎 {\mathbf{e}}^{-}_{\theta}\leftarrow{\mathbf{e}}^{\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta} , removing both clean context and SC. Experimental setup. 
We compare the two choices using the same Sigma - unreg -3B checkpoint with block length 32 32 , full GSM8K and HumanEval evaluation sets, and 10 samples per problem. The DDIM sweep uses 64 64 steps per block, uniform- γ \gamma schedule, and ST τ = 0.3 \tau=0.3 ; the DDPM sweep uses 256 256 steps per block, EDM ρ = 7 \rho=7 schedule, and ST τ = 1 \tau=1 . We remark that the guidance is inactive at w = 1 w=1 and the two choices coincide. Table 12 : GSM8K accuracy (%) for self-conditioning auto-guidance.
“Context/no-SC” retains clean context but removes SC (i.e., 𝐞 θ − ← 𝐞 θ 𝐜 , 𝟎 {\mathbf{e}}^{-}_{\theta}\leftarrow{\mathbf{e}}^{{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta} ); ours removes both (i.e., 𝐞 θ − ← 𝐞 θ ∅ , 𝟎 {\mathbf{e}}^{-}_{\theta}\leftarrow{\mathbf{e}}^{\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta} ). As shown in Tabs. 12 and 13 , the fully unconditional bad prediction (ours) is consistently stronger in the useful guidance range:
it almost wins every matched CFG weight in sweep on both tasks and both samplers, and, tuned over the sweep, reaches a higher ceiling in each task.
Thus, we conclude that the advantage shown here is not an artifact of using different guidance search ranges. 
E.6 Ablation Study: Steering in Logit Space instead of Embedding Space 
[Back to Sec. 4.2 in the main paper.] 
Both steering knobs introduced in Sec. 4.2 act on the predicted clean embedding 𝐞 θ ∈ ℝ L ′ × d e {\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{L^{\prime}\times d_{e}} , equivalently on the score or velocity. Recall that our denoiser leverages categorical reparameterization , i.e., 𝐞 θ = softmax ⁡ ( ℓ θ ) ​ 𝐄 = 𝐱 θ ​ 𝐄 {\mathbf{e}}_{\theta}=\operatorname{softmax}({\bm{\ell}}_{\theta}){\mathbf{E}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}} , where ℓ θ ∈ ℝ L ′ × V {\bm{\ell}}_{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{L^{\prime}\times V} are the predicted logits, and softmax \operatorname{softmax} operates in the vocabulary dimension. Thus, a natural alternative is to apply the same operations to ℓ θ {\bm{\ell}}_{\theta} before the softmax \operatorname{softmax} . This is the form of guidance used by discrete dLMs, which does not have embedding-space prediction to steer ( Nie et al., 2025 ; Li et al., 2025 ; Schiff et al., 2025 ) ( App. A ). Logit-space CFG (LCFG). 
Reusing the notations in Sec. E.5 , with the same fully unconditional bad prediction, we do softmax on the extrapolated logits: 
𝐞 θ w , logit := softmax ⁡ ( ℓ θ ∅ , 𝟎 + w ⁡ [ ℓ θ + − ℓ θ ∅ , 𝟎 ] ) ​ 𝐄 , w ≥ 1 , \displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}^{w,\text{logit}}_{\theta}:=\operatorname{softmax}({\bm{\ell}}^{\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta}+w\,[{\bm{\ell}}^{+}_{\theta}-{\bm{\ell}}^{\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta}]){\mathbf{E}},\qquad w\geq 1, 
against our default 𝐞 θ w = 𝐞 θ ∅ , 𝟎 + w ⁡ [ 𝐞 θ + − 𝐞 θ ∅ , 𝟎 ] {\mathbf{e}}^{w}_{\theta}={\mathbf{e}}^{\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta}+w[{\mathbf{e}}^{+}_{\theta}-{\mathbf{e}}^{\varnothing,{\mathbf{0}}}_{\theta}] of CFG , which extrapolates after the softmax. The two coincide at w = 1 w=1 (i.e., no guidance). Logit temperature (LT). 
This is the analogue of ST in logit space, which rescales the conditional logits themselves: 
𝐞 θ T := softmax ⁡ ( ℓ θ + T ) ​ 𝐄 , T ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] , \displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}^{T}_{\theta}:=\operatorname{softmax}\Big(\frac{{\bm{\ell}}^{+}_{\theta}}{T}\Big){\mathbf{E}},\qquad T\in[0,1], 
where T = 1 T=1 recovers the conditional mean embedding prediction (i.e., no guidance), and smaller T T sharpens. The T → 0 T\to 0 limit is the maximum a posteriori (MAP) denoiser 𝐞 θ 0 = 𝐄 ⁡ [ argmax ℓ θ + ] {\mathbf{e}}^{0}_{\theta}={\mathbf{E}}[\argmax{\bm{\ell}}^{+}_{\theta}] , which snaps the prediction onto a single vocabulary embedding. 
We remark that both operations change the trajectory only; tokens are still read out as the argmax \argmax of the conditional logits at the final step t = 0 t=0 , exactly as in Secs. 4.1 and 2 . Also, LCFG does not cost extra compute compared with the embedding-space counterpart, since we need to get both conditional and unconditional logit predictions. Experimental setup. 
We use the setting identical to the default in Sec. E.5 . We use the same Sigma - unreg -3B checkpoint with block length 32 32 , the full GSM8K and HumanEval evaluation sets, and generate 10 responses per problem. DDIM uses 64 64 steps/block on a uniform- γ \gamma time discretization with τ = 0.3 \tau=0.3 , and DDPM uses 256 256 steps/block on the EDM ρ = 7 \rho=7 time discretization and τ = 1 \tau=1 . Table 14 : Logit-space versus embedding-space CFG. GSM8K is accuracy (%) and HumanEval is execution-based pass@ 1 1 (%). Entries are mean ± \pm two standard errors over the 10 generations per prompt, an approximate 95 % 95\% confidence interval for the mean, and bold marks the best entry in each row. The last column is the best value over the full sweep of Tabs. 12 and 13 , which continues past w = 5 w=5 for the embedding rows. The logit rows were swept only to w = 5 w=5 as they already peak at w = 2 w=2 and fall monotonically after that.

Logit-space CFG (LCFG) peaks at w = 2 w=2 in all four settings, whereas embedding-space CFG improves up to w = 4 ∼ 10 w=4\sim 10 ; at their respective optima, LCFG gains only 0.30 0.30 points on GSM8K/DDIM and loses 2.44 ∼ 17.26 2.44\sim 17.26 points elsewhere ( Tab. 14 ). Logit temperature (LT) provides effective sharpening without a second branch: on DDPM, lowering T T from 1 1 to 1 / 5 1/5 raises GSM8K from 52.44 52.44 to 79.11 79.11 , while the MAP endpoint T = 0 T=0 retains 78.61 78.61 ( Tab. 15 ). Relative to matched sharpening ( T = 1 / w T=1/w ), the unconditional branch in LCFG contributes negligibly at w = 2 w=2 and hurts at larger weights. LT also complements ST, adding 9.42 9.42 points on DDIM with ST = 0.3 =0.3 , but reduces accuracy when combined with both CFG and ST ( Tab. 16 ). We therefore retain embedding-space CFG and ST as the default choice. 
E.7 Ablation Study: Commit-Stride Sampling 
[Back to Sec. 4 in the main paper.] 
Beyond blockwise decoding of L ′ L^{\prime} tokens per denoising window, we further introduce a commit stride S ∈ { 1 , … , L ′ } S\in\{1,\dots,L^{\prime}\} .
Each denoising window still runs the same blockwise reverse process over a block of fresh Gaussian noise with length L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 , but only the leading S ∈ { 8 , 16 , 32 } S\in\{8,16,32\} tokens are committed back into the causal KV cache; the remaining L ′ − S L^{\prime}-S tail positions are discarded and re-drawn as fresh noise in the next window (which is shifted forward by S S absolute positions). Setting S ← L ′ S\leftarrow L^{\prime} recovers the default block-decoding method.
The pseudocode for commit stride can be found in Alg. 2 .
This is a pure inference-time technique that further improves the output quality even under matched compute budgets, as we will see below. Setup. 
We evaluate on the base models of Tabs. 1 and 2 : Sigma - unreg -3B/8B, both with block size L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 . Decoding invariants match the diffusion-mode rows of Tab. 1 . For each stride, CFG w w and ST τ \tau are chosen based on the number of DDIM steps per window ( Tab. 10 ): ( w , τ ) = ( 3 , 0.2 ) (w,\tau)=(3,0.2) at ≥ 64 \geq 64 steps, ( 3 , 0.3 ) (3,0.3) at 32 32 steps, ( 3 , 0.5 ) (3,0.5) at 16 16 steps, and ( 3 , 0.8 ) (3,0.8) at 8 8 steps. All cells generate 10 10 samples for each prompt and report mean ± \pm two standard errors ( Sec. E.3 ). Nominal vs. true NFE. 
For simplicity, we match compute cost by nominal NFE, defined as the number of denoiser forward passes required to commit L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 tokens under the given (steps, stride) pair: 
Nominal NFE := N ​ L ′ S , \text{Nominal\penalty\ NFE}\;:=\;\frac{NL^{\prime}}{S}, 
where N N is the number of diffusion steps per denoising window.
Since every denoising window operates on the same L ′ L^{\prime} -wide noisy block appended to the same-length clean prefix, one forward pass costs the same FLOPs regardless of S S . We present the results for commit-stride sampling for Sigma - unreg -3B/8B in Tabs. 17 and 18 , respectively. The tables sweep all three strides S ∈ { 8 , 16 , 32 } S\in\{8,16,32\} within each nominal-NFE bucket { 256,128 , 64 , 32 } \{256,128,64,32\} . At the top NFE (nominal 256 256 ), the DDIM 64 64 steps / stride 8 8 row is the best on both models: it beats the tuned DDPM-256 baseline of Tabs. 1 and 2 by + 2.66 +2.66 average points on 3B and + 0.76 +0.76 on 8B, with the largest per-task gains on the coding benchmarks such as MBPP. The same ordering holds down to around nominal 64 64 : committing fewer tokens per window (i.e., smaller stride S S ) improves the average monotonically. At nominal 128 128 NFE, DDIM 32 32 steps / stride 8 8 reaches within a point of the top budget on both sizes, and still above the full-stride DDPM-256 baselines. The benefit of a smaller stride disappears once it forces the per-window step count below roughly 16 16 : at nominal 32 32 , stride 8 8 leaves only 8 8 steps per window, and its coarse time grid collapses quality due to larger discretization error, falling below the stride- 16 16 and stride- 32 32 rows at the same budget.

We measure the wall-clock cost of the packed CFG forward with attention mask ( Fig. 5(a) ) at batch size 1 1 compared with the unguided forward. We decode the same set of 16 16 GSM8K questions ( 8 8 -shot, prompt length 729 ∼ 814 729\sim 814 ) with the DDIM sampler at 32 32 steps per block, on the first block only.
Each question therefore requires 32 + 1 32+1 forward passes ( Alg. 2 ).
The very first denoiser query of each process involves a one-time CUDA initialization which takes a longer time, so is excluded to avoid biasing the measurement. All runs are performed on one NVIDIA H100 GPU, using Sigma - unreg -3B with precision BF16 . 
Results in Tab. 19 show that at L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 the packed transformer forward costs + 1.8 % +1.8\% over the unguided one, and at L ′ = 64 L^{\prime}=64 it is free to within measurement noise. The per-NFE overhead is dominated by computing the second set of logits, doing softmax, and multiplying by the embedding matrix rather than by attention. 
E.10 Reasoning Trajectory Analysis 
[Back to Sec. 4.4 in the main paper.] 
As noted in Tab. 2 , we empirically observe less left-to-right bias within a block for Sigma compared with the confidence-based masked dLM sampling. We demonstrate this in Fig. 19 , where we show a commit order analysis between continuous and discrete dLMs. Figure 19 : Token commitment order vs token position for Sigma - unreg -3B Sigma uses 256-step DDPM ( w = 2 , τ = 0.1 w=2,\tau=0.1 ) as in Tab. 1 . Generation order averaged across multiple documents and blocks. For Sigma - unreg -3B, the commitment step t i t_{i} of position i i is defined as the smallest t t such that for every step t ′ ≥ t t^{\prime}\geq t up to the final readout, (i) the argmax of 𝐱 θ \mathbf{x}_{\theta} equals the finally decoded token, and (ii) that argmax is assigned probability at least 0.9 0.9 . The commitment order of position i i is the rank of t i t_{i} among t 1 , … , t 32 {t_{1},\dots,t_{32}} within its block, in ascending order. Positions sharing a commitment step share the lowest rank of the tie. 
Appendix F Implementation Details and Additional Results for Few-step Distillation 
F.1 Method and Training Objective 
We initialize a student with parameters ϕ \phi from the teacher parameters θ \theta , and devise a continuous relaxation of Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD, Yin et al. (2024b) ) for language models, inspired by Le Van Kiem et al. (2026) and Fan et al. (2026) , to train the student to estimate a clean-context-conditional teacher-generated data distribution p teacher ( ⋅ ∣ 𝐜 ) p_{\text{teacher}}(\cdot\mid{\mathbf{c}}) . In the following we omit the context conditioning for readability.
As detailed in Sec. F.2 , this distribution is obtained using the strongest teacher sampler for optimal distributional quality (high NFE and guidance).
We choose p teacher p_{\text{teacher}} as the target distribution instead of using the pretraining dataset, as we observed that distillation performance was better for the former, likely due to the inductive bias existing in self-distillation since the student is initialized from the teacher.
We ablate this choice along with other design decisions in Sec. F.3 . 
We train the student on-policy for a specific number of NFEs M M , i.e., the student should produce its own multi-step mapping from the Gaussian prior to the target data distribution by matching intermediate noisy marginals, following the principle in Fan et al. (2026) .
The training procedure is detailed in Alg. 3 . 
At each training iteration,
we pick a clean target sample 𝐞 ∼ p teacher {\mathbf{e}}\sim p_{\text{teacher}} and independently initialize the rollout with a near-standard-Gaussian state 𝐞 1 ∼ q 1 ​ ( 𝟎 , γ 1 ) {\mathbf{e}}_{1}\sim q_{1}({\mathbf{0}},\gamma_{1}) .
The student is rolled out for M s ∼ Unif ⁡ { 0 , … , M − 1 } M_{s}\sim\operatorname{Unif}\{0,\dots,M-1\} steps from 𝐞 1 {\mathbf{e}}_{1} until a source state 𝐞 s {\mathbf{e}}_{s} with noise level γ s = γ 1 − M s M ​ ( γ 1 − γ 0 ) \gamma_{s}=\gamma_{1}-\frac{M_{s}}{M}(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{0}) using self-conditioning.
The multi-step sampler we use for rollout as well as during inference is the consistency-model-style procedure used in Yin et al. (2024a) ; Song et al. (2023) : 
𝐞 t n − 1 = α t n + 1 ​ 𝐞 0 ϕ , t n + σ t n + 1 ​ ϵ 𝐞 0 ϕ , t n = 𝐞 ϕ ​ ( 𝐞 t n , γ t n ) ϵ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 ) , {\mathbf{e}}_{t_{n-1}}=\alpha_{t_{n+1}}{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,t_{n}}+\sigma_{t_{n+1}}{\bm{\epsilon}}\qquad{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,t_{n}}={\mathbf{e}}_{\phi}({\mathbf{e}}_{t_{n}},\gamma_{t_{n}})\qquad{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{I}})\,, (17) 
This means the intermediate states have the exact same noise marginals as the forward variance-preserving process, but the state is centered around the current clean estimate.
We detach this prefix rollout and treat its source-state distribution as fixed during the selected student update: the student objective is not differentiated through the earlier rollout steps. γ ¯ j ← γ 1 − j ​ Δ ​ γ \bar{\gamma}_{j}\leftarrow\gamma_{1}-j\Delta\gamma for j = 0 , … , M j=0,\ldots,M ⊳ \triangleright Uniform logNSR discretization 4: Reference pre-fitting 5: for n = 1 , … , N ref-fitting n=1,\dots,N_{\text{ref-fitting}} do 6: γ u ∼ Unif ⁡ [ γ 0 , γ 1 ] \gamma_{u}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}] ; 𝐞 u ∼ q u ​ ( 𝐞 ∣ γ u ) {\mathbf{e}}_{u}\sim q_{u}({\mathbf{e}}\mid\gamma_{u}) 7: ℒ ref ψ ← CE ⁡ ( 𝐱 , 𝐱 ψ ​ ( 𝐞 u , γ u ) ) \mathcal{L}^{\psi}_{\mathrm{ref}}\leftarrow\mathrm{CE}\big({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{x}}_{\psi}({\mathbf{e}}_{u},\gamma_{u})\big) ⊳ \triangleright Reference loss equation 21 8: ψ ← AdamW ​ ( ℒ ref ψ ) \psi\leftarrow\text{AdamW}(\mathcal{L}^{\psi}_{\mathrm{ref}}) ⊳ \triangleright Reference update 9: end for 10: while not converged do 11: 𝐱 ∼ p teacher {\mathbf{x}}\sim p_{\mathrm{teacher}} ; 𝐞 ← 𝐱𝐄 {\mathbf{e}}\leftarrow{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{E}} ⊳ \triangleright Sample target data 12: Student rollout 13: M s ∼ Unif ⁡ { 0 , … , M − 1 } M_{s}\sim\operatorname{Unif}\{0,\ldots,M-1\} ⊳ \triangleright Sample rollout depth 14: 𝐞 s ← σ ⁡ ( γ 1 ) ​ ϵ {\mathbf{e}}_{s}\leftarrow\sigma(\gamma_{1}){\bm{\epsilon}} , ϵ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 ) {\bm{\epsilon}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{I}}) ; 𝐞 SC ← ∅ {\mathbf{e}}_{\mathrm{SC}}\leftarrow\varnothing ⊳ \triangleright Initialize rollout states 15: for j = 0 , … , M s − 1 j=0,\ldots,M_{s}-1 do 16: 𝐞 ^ 0 ← 𝐞 ϕ ​ ( 𝐞 s , γ ¯ j , 𝐞 SC ) \widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{0}\leftarrow{\mathbf{e}}_{\phi}({\mathbf{e}}_{s},\bar{\gamma}_{j};{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathrm{SC}}) ⊳ \triangleright Rollout transition with carried self-conditioning 17: 𝐞 s ← α ⁡ ( γ ¯ j + 1 ) ​ 𝐞 ^ 0 + σ ⁡ ( γ ¯ j + 1 ) ​ ϵ {\mathbf{e}}_{s}\leftarrow\alpha(\bar{\gamma}_{j+1})\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{0}+\sigma(\bar{\gamma}_{j+1}){\bm{\epsilon}} , ϵ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 ) {\bm{\epsilon}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{I}}) ⊳ \triangleright Consistency-style transition 18: 𝐞 SC ← 𝐞 ^ 0 {\mathbf{e}}_{\mathrm{SC}}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{0} ⊳ \triangleright Update self-conditioning 19: end for 20: γ s ← γ ¯ M s \gamma_{s}\leftarrow\bar{\gamma}_{M_{s}} ; γ r ← γ ¯ M s + 1 \gamma_{r}\leftarrow\bar{\gamma}_{M_{s}+1} ⊳ \triangleright Segment start and end points 21: 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ← 𝐞 ϕ ​ ( sg ⁡ [ 𝐞 s ] , γ s ) {\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}\leftarrow{\mathbf{e}}_{\phi}(\operatorname{sg}[{\mathbf{e}}_{s}],\gamma_{s}) ⊳ \triangleright Student prediction 22: Auxiliary updates 23: for k = 1 , … , 4 k=1,\ldots,4 do 24: γ t ∼ Unif ⁡ [ γ r , γ s ] \gamma_{t}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[\gamma_{r},\gamma_{s}] ; 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∼ q t ​ ( sg ⁡ [ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ] ∣ γ t ) {\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s}\sim q_{t}(\operatorname{sg}[{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}]\mid\gamma_{t}) ⊳ \triangleright Re-noise student sample to query point 25: ℒ aux η ← h t ​ CE ​ ( sg ⁡ [ 𝐱 ϕ , s ] , 𝐞 η ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ) \mathcal{L}^{\eta}_{\mathrm{aux}}\leftarrow h_{t}\,\mathrm{CE}\big(\operatorname{sg}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\phi,s}],{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})\big) ⊳ \triangleright Auxiliary loss equation 20 26: η ← AdamW ​ ( ℒ aux η ) \eta\leftarrow\text{AdamW}(\mathcal{L}^{\eta}_{\text{aux}}) ⊳ \triangleright Auxiliary update 27: end for 28: Student update 29: γ t ∼ Unif ⁡ [ γ r , γ s ] \gamma_{t}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[\gamma_{r},\gamma_{s}] ; 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∼ q t ​ ( sg ⁡ [ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ] ∣ γ t ) {\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s}\sim q_{t}(\operatorname{sg}[{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}]\mid\gamma_{t}) ⊳ \triangleright Re-noise student sample to query point 30: 𝐞 ^ η ← 𝐞 η ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) \widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}\leftarrow{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t}) ; 𝐞 ^ ψ ← 𝐞 ψ ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) \widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}\leftarrow{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t}) ⊳ \triangleright Auxiliary and reference denoising 31: ℒ DMD2 ​ - ​ grad ϕ ← 1 R ​ ∑ l = 1 L ′ ⟨ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s , l , sg ⁡ [ 𝐞 ^ η − 𝐞 ^ ψ ] ⟩ \mathcal{L}^{\phi}_{\mathrm{DMD2\text{-}grad}}\leftarrow\frac{1}{R}\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{L^{\prime}}\big\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s,l},\operatorname{sg}\big[\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}-\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}\big]\big\rangle ⊳ \triangleright Student loss equation 22 32: ϕ ← AdamW ​ ( ℒ DMD2 ​ - ​ grad ϕ ) \phi\leftarrow\text{AdamW}(\mathcal{L}^{\phi}_{\mathrm{DMD2\text{-}grad}}) ⊳ \triangleright Student update 33: end while 34: return ϕ \phi 
From the given source state 𝐞 s {\mathbf{e}}_{s} , the student is called to produce a clean embedding estimate 𝐞 0 ϕ , s := 𝐞 ϕ ​ ( 𝐞 s , γ s ) {\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}:={\mathbf{e}}_{\phi}({\mathbf{e}}_{s},\gamma_{s}) , which is re-noised to a query noise level sampled uniformly within the segment starting from the source noise level γ t ∼ Unif ⁡ [ γ 1 − M s + 1 M ​ ( γ 1 − γ 0 ) , γ 1 − M s M ​ ( γ 1 − γ 0 ) ] \gamma_{t}\sim\operatorname{Unif}\Big[\gamma_{1}-\frac{M_{s}+1}{M}(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{0}),\gamma_{1}-\frac{M_{s}}{M}(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{0})\Big] .
This together yields the re-noised student sample 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∼ q t ​ ( 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∣ γ t ) {\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t}\sim q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}\mid\gamma_{t}) and we denote the resulting marginal as q t ϕ , s q^{\phi,s}_{t} .
Similarly, the target embedding 𝐞 {\mathbf{e}} is re-noised to the same query level, yielding a sample 𝐞 t ∼ q t ​ ( 𝐞 , γ t ) {\mathbf{e}}_{t}\sim q_{t}({\mathbf{e}},\gamma_{t}) . 
These choices of source and query distribution correspond to a specific choice of components described in Fan et al. (2026) .
Like Fan et al. (2026) , we train our students in an on-policy fashion; however, we use fully stochastic transitions as in Yin et al. (2024a) ; Le Van Kiem et al. (2026) whereas Fan et al. (2026) used deterministic DDIM transitions. Furthermore, we do not use phase experts but only one set of student weights.
As we ablate it later, this specific choice of source and query noise level distributions has a large influence on the overall student quality. 
We match the marginals q t ϕ , s q^{\phi,s}_{t} and q t q_{t} using reverse-KL, which smooths out the training objective compared to directly trying to match clean embedding distributions ( Yin et al., 2024b ) : 
𝒦 s , t ( ϕ ) = KL ( q t ϕ , s ∥ q t ) , ℒ DMD ϕ = 𝔼 γ s , γ t [ 𝒦 s , t ( ϕ ) ] . \mathcal{K}_{s,t}(\phi)=\operatorname{KL}\big(q^{\phi,s}_{t}\,\|\,q_{t}\big)\,,\qquad\mathcal{L}^{\phi}_{\text{DMD}}=\E_{\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t}}\big[\mathcal{K}_{s,t}(\phi)\big]\,. (18) 
Deriving the gradient of ℒ DMD ϕ \mathcal{L}^{\phi}_{\text{DMD}} ( Le Van Kiem et al., 2026 ) yields the following gradient surrogate loss: 
ℒ DMD-grad ϕ = 𝔼 γ t , γ s , 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∼ q t ​ ( 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∣ γ t ) ⁡ [ e − γ t ​ ∑ l = 1 L ′ ⟨ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s , l , sg ⁡ [ 𝐞 η l ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) − 𝐞 ψ l ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ] ⟩ ] , \mathcal{L}^{\phi}_{\text{DMD-grad}}=\E_{\gamma_{t},\gamma_{s},{\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t}\sim q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{0}\mid\gamma_{t})}\Big[\;{\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma_{t}}\sum_{l=1}^{L^{\prime}}\big\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s,l},\operatorname{sg}\big[{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}^{l}({\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}^{l}({\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t},\gamma_{t})\big]\big\rangle\Big]\,, (19) 
with reference denoiser 𝐞 ψ {\mathbf{e}}_{\psi} (linked to the so-called “real score estimator” in the literature) and the auxiliary denoiser 𝐞 η {\mathbf{e}}_{\eta} (linked to the “fake score estimator” in the literature).
The full derivation is presented in Sec. F.4 .
The reference denoiser 𝐞 ψ {\mathbf{e}}_{\psi} denoises the re-noised student’s samples toward the target distribution p teacher p_{\text{teacher}} , while the auxiliary denoiser 𝐞 η {\mathbf{e}}_{\eta} denoises the re-noised student’s samples toward the student distribution. Steering the student with the detached difference of these two denoisers in 19 matches the student and target distributions.
Following Le Van Kiem et al. (2026) , we train the categorical auxiliary denoiser with a soft-target cross-entropy against the detached student-produced probabilities: 
ℒ aux η = 𝔼 γ t , γ s , 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∼ q t ​ ( 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∣ γ t ) [ − ∑ l = 1 L ′ ⟨ sg [ 𝐱 ϕ , s , l ] , log 𝐱 η l ( sg [ 𝐞 t ϕ , s ] , γ t ) ⟩ ] , \mathcal{L}^{\eta}_{\text{aux}}=\E_{\gamma_{t},\gamma_{s},{\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t}\sim q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{0}\mid\gamma_{t})}\Big[-\sum_{l=1}^{L^{\prime}}\big\langle\operatorname{sg}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\phi,s,l}]\,,\,\log{\mathbf{x}}^{l}_{\eta}(\operatorname{sg}[{\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t}],\gamma_{t})\big\rangle\Big]\,, (20) 
with 𝐞 η = 𝐱 η ​ 𝐄 {\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}={\mathbf{x}}_{\eta}{\mathbf{E}} .
Usually the teacher diffusion model serves directly as 𝐞 ψ {\mathbf{e}}_{\psi} since it is trained as a denoiser of the pretraining data distribution p data p_{\mathrm{data}} .
However, since our target distribution is p teacher p_{\text{teacher}} , i.e., teacher-generated samples rather than the pretraining distribution, we re-fit the reference denoiser (initialized from the teacher) to p teacher p_{\text{teacher}} using cross-entropy: 
ℒ ref ψ = 𝔼 γ u ∼ Unif ⁡ [ γ 0 , γ 1 ] , 𝐞 ∼ p teacher , 𝐞 u ∼ q t ​ ( 𝐞 ∣ γ u ) [ − ∑ l = 1 L ′ ⟨ 𝐱 l , log 𝐱 ψ l ( 𝐞 u , γ u ) ⟩ ] , \mathcal{L}^{\psi}_{\text{ref}}=\E_{\gamma_{u}\sim\operatorname{Unif}[\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}],{\mathbf{e}}\sim p_{\text{teacher}},{\mathbf{e}}_{u}\sim q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}\mid\gamma_{u})}\Big[-\sum_{l=1}^{L^{\prime}}\big\langle{\mathbf{x}}^{l},\,\log{\mathbf{x}}^{l}_{\psi}({\mathbf{e}}_{u},\gamma_{u})\big\rangle\Big]\,, (21) 
with 𝐞 = 𝐱𝐄 {\mathbf{e}}={\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{E}} and 𝐞 ψ = 𝐱 ψ ​ 𝐄 {\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}={\mathbf{x}}_{\psi}{\mathbf{E}} . 
The auxiliary denoiser, the reference denoiser and the main student all use same-state two-call training for self-conditioning ( Chen et al., 2023 ) with probability p SC = 0.25 p_{\text{SC}}=0.25 while inference carries the previous raw clean prediction, thereby mimicking the original teacher’s training and inference behaviors.
The complete training procedure is summarized in Alg. 3 . 
F.2 Implementation Details Target distribution. 
The target distribution is a teacher-generated bank
containing 65,536 clean prefixes of 256 tokens, taken from the pretraining dataset,
and four teacher-generated
32-token continuation blocks per prefix.
This bank is generated with the empirically optimal teacher sampler, i.e.,
256-step DDPM sampling with self-conditioning enabled and optimal guidance: for Sigma -3B this is CFG w = 2 w=2 and ST τ = 0.1 \tau=0.1 , whereas for Sigma -8B this is CFG w = 3 w=3 and ST τ = 0.1 \tau=0.1 . Parameterization and optimization. 
All three neural networks are initialized from the same respective teacher checkpoint,
with separate trainable parameter copies and optimizers. We copy the embedding matrix, noise schedule and noise endpoints from the teacher and retain them throughout
distillation.
We detach the source rollout and the SC preliminary
prediction, so student gradients pass only through the selected
denoising transition, similar to Fan et al. (2026) .
We prefit the reference denoiser 𝐞 ψ {\mathbf{e}}_{\psi} for 50 optimizer updates before starting student/auxiliary updates. Each subsequent student update is preceded by four auxiliary updates
and four reference updates.
We optimize all networks with AdamW with a global batch size of 256, β 1 = 0.9 \beta_{1}=0.9 , β 2 = 0.999 \beta_{2}=0.999 , zero
weight decay, and unit-norm gradient clipping. The learning rates for each network are selected through gradient balancing and are 3.6 × 10 − 7 3.6\times 10^{-7} for the student, 1.188 × 10 − 5 1.188\times 10^{-5} for the auxiliary denoiser, and 10 − 6 10^{-6} for the reference denoiser. 
In practice, we use the residual-normalization strategy in Yin et al. (2024a) and remove the SNR weight e − γ t {\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma_{t}} , thereby replacing 19 with 
ℒ DMD2-grad ϕ = 𝔼 γ t , γ s , 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∼ q t ​ ( 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∣ γ t ) ⁡ [ 1 R ​ ∑ l = 1 L ′ ⟨ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s , l , sg ⁡ [ 𝐞 η l ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) − 𝐞 ψ l ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ] ⟩ ] , \mathcal{L}^{\phi}_{\text{DMD2-grad}}=\E_{\gamma_{t},\gamma_{s},{\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t}\sim q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{0}\mid\gamma_{t})}\Big[\;\frac{1}{R}\sum_{l=1}^{L^{\prime}}\big\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s,l},\operatorname{sg}\big[{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}^{l}({\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}^{l}({\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t},\gamma_{t})\big]\big\rangle\Big]\,, (22) 
with the student-to-reference denoiser residual 
R = 10 − 6 + 1 L ′ ​ d e ​ ∑ l = 1 L ′ sg ⁡ [ ‖ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s , l − 𝐞 ψ l ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ‖ 1 ] . R=10^{-6}+\frac{1}{L^{\prime}d_{e}}\sum_{l=1}^{L^{\prime}}\operatorname{sg}\big[\big\|{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s,l}-{\mathbf{e}}^{l}_{\psi}({\mathbf{e}}^{\phi,s}_{t},\gamma_{t})\big\|_{1}\big]\,. (23) 
We further stabilize the auxiliary denoiser training by multiplying its objective in 20 with 
h t = min ⁡ ( 1 , 10 ​ σ 2 ​ ( γ t ) ​ ( 1 − e γ r − γ t ) ) , h_{t}=\min(1,\,10\,\sigma^{2}(\gamma_{t})(1-{\mathrm{e}}^{\gamma_{r}-\gamma_{t}}))\,, (24) 
where γ r \gamma_{r} is the cleaner endpoint of the
selected segment. 
F.3 Additional Results Diversity and pass@ k k . 
We demonstrate here that our distribution-matching distillation procedure preserves the diversity of the target teacher distribution, i.e., we do not fall into the pitfall often encountered in DMD methods.
We use 512 prompts and sample 8 trajectories per prompt, on 3 benchmarks (GSM8K, MBPP and Novelty-Bench).
GSM8K measures math reasoning, MBPP measures coding ability, and Novelty-Bench ( Zhang et al., 2025 ) is an open-ended creative writing benchmark which helps us measure actual text diversity and creativity. 
We measure pass@ 1 1 and pass@ 8 8 accuracies as well as semantic diversity using the Vendi score ( Friedman and Dieng, 2023 ) . We compute the Vendi score as the exponentiated Jensen-Shannon entropy of the pairwise similarity matrix between SentenceBERT embeddings of the produced outputs.
Results displayed in Figs. 20 , 21 and 22 clearly demonstrate that our DMD student recovers the DDPM-256 Vendi score as well as its pass@ 8 8 accuracy, with as few as 4 diffusion steps. Furthermore, the DDIM-32 Sigma -3B (with self-conditioning) teacher has lower diversity and pass@ 8 8 accuracy than both our Sigma - distill -3B students and the DDPM-256 Sigma -3B, although its pass@ 1 1 is somewhat comparable to our 4-NFE Sigma - distill -3B.
This suggests that our distilled students balance quality and diversity for only a fraction of the inference cost, which is very promising for, e.g., RL-based post-training. Figure 20 : Diversity and pass@ k k results on GSM8K 512-prompt subset. Figure 21 : Diversity and pass@ k k results on MBPP 500-prompt test set. Figure 22 : Diversity and pass@ k k results on NoveltyBench and NoveltyBench-WildCat 512-prompt test set. Ablation 1: Teacher. 
We investigate the influence of the teacher’s geometry on the distillation performance. We observed earlier that improving the regularity of the geometry is key for low-NFE performance. Indeed, the unregularized Sigma - unreg -3B teacher exhibits local embedding collapse, lowering the global rank participation and creating uncertainty during decoding, which particularly affects coding tasks (see Secs. 4.3 and 10 ).
This consequence transfers naturally to the distillation quality, as we notice in Fig. 23 that Sigma -3B teaches much stronger students at 4- and 8-NFE in particular. Figure 23 : Teacher ablation for DMD distillation at 3B scale. Global batch size 64. Ablation 2: Global Batch Size. 
We ablate the effect of global batch size on distillation quality. Increasing the global batch size from 64 to 256 yields large and consistent improvements across NFEs. Increasing batch size above 256 did not further improve results. Figure 24 : Global batch size ablation for DMD distillation from Sigma -3B teacher. Ablation 3: Query Distributions. 
Choosing the query distribution is a key design choice in DMD distillation. After obtaining very good early results with Phased DMD ( Fan et al., 2026 ) , we ablated the discrepancies between Phased DMD and plain DMD, and noticed that the source states, the transitions and the source and query distributions were the most discriminative factors. As we kept the consistency-style transitions with fresh Gaussian noise and the student rollout for obtaining our input states, the main remaining difference was the query distribution.
We choose three candidate distributions: Uniform, Gumbel fitted to the teacher’s empirical denoising cross-entropy distribution (skewed toward intermediate noise levels), and β ⁡ ( 2.5 , 1 ) \beta(2.5,1) (skewed toward high noise levels); as well as three ranges for the distribution support: the whole range [ γ 0 , γ 1 ] [\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}] , within segment [ γ r , γ t ] [\gamma_{r},\gamma_{t}] , and above segment [ γ t , γ 1 ] [\gamma_{t},\gamma_{1}] .
We conduct this ablation specifically on the 4-NFE distillation setup.
In Fig. 25 , we observe that using the current segment as the support consistently obtained the best results, and that the exact distribution did not matter so much there. The teacher-CE-fit Gumbel above segment obtained excellent results too, but we noticed that the resulting distillation procedure was very unstable. Figure 25 : Query distribution ablation for DMD distillation from Sigma -3B teacher. Global batch size 64. M = 4 M=4 NFE distillation. Ablation 4: Self-conditioning and student input. 
Using self-conditioning in the student is absolutely key to its generation quality.
The self-conditioning probability used during distillation hardly influences the result: for simplicity we retain p SC = 0.25 p_{\text{SC}}=0.25 .
Using p SC = 0 p_{\text{SC}}=0 degraded results slightly, but far worse is deactivating self-conditioning at inference, as shown in Fig. 26 . Figure 26 : Self-conditioning ablation for DMD distillation from Sigma -3B teacher. Global batch size 64. Ablation 5: Training data. 
We observe in Fig. 27 that teacher-generated data led to better results than directly using the pretraining dataset. We posit this is due to the inductive bias carried by the student about the target distribution, since it is initialized from the teacher that produced this distribution.
We conduct this ablation specifically on the 4-NFE distillation setup. Figure 27 : Data distribution ablation for DMD distillation from Sigma -3B teacher. Global batch size 64. M = 4 M=4 NFE distillation. Ablation 6: Student and auxiliary objectives. 
We ablate the choice of student loss weighting (“Norm off SNR on” 19 vs. “Norm on SNR off” 22 ) and the auxiliary denoiser parameterization and objective.
“Soft CE” indicates our baseline, i.e., a categorical denoiser (similar to the teacher) trained with cross-entropy on soft probabilities. “MSE” indicates a denoiser augmented with an MLP-parameterized residual connection from Transformer final hidden state to the re-embedded categorical prediction, trained with MSE in the embedding space directly.
We conduct this ablation specifically on the 4-NFE distillation setup.
The results highlighted in Fig. 28 favor the Soft CE auxiliary objective introduced in Le Van Kiem et al. (2026) ; the DMD2-type normalization ( Yin et al., 2024a ) does not play a major role, although we informally observed it led to faster and more stable convergence, therefore being favored in our final setup. Figure 28 : Student objective ablation for DMD distillation from Sigma -3B teacher. Global batch size 64. M = 4 M=4 NFE distillation. 
F.4 Proof: DMD Gradient Surrogate Loss 
In this section, we prove the form of the DMD gradient surrogate loss in 19 . Proof. 
We first fix the source and query noise levels ( γ s , γ t ) (\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t}) .
All distributions below are implicitly conditioned on the clean prefix.
For this derivation, we hold the source-state sampling distribution
fixed, consistently with detaching the on-policy source rollout. 
Write α t = α ⁡ ( γ t ) \alpha_{t}=\alpha(\gamma_{t}) and σ t = σ ⁡ ( γ t ) \sigma_{t}=\sigma(\gamma_{t}) .
The Gaussian corruption admits the reparameterization 
𝐞 t ϕ , s = α t ​ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s + σ t ​ ϵ , 𝐞 0 ϕ , s = 𝐞 ϕ ​ ( 𝐞 s , γ s ) , ϵ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 ) . {\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s}=\alpha_{t}{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}+\sigma_{t}{\bm{\epsilon}},\qquad{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}={\mathbf{e}}_{\phi}({\mathbf{e}}_{s},\gamma_{s}),\qquad{\bm{\epsilon}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{I}}). (25) 
Our log-noise-to-signal parameterization satisfies 
γ t = log ⁡ σ t 2 α t 2 , α t 2 σ t 2 = e − γ t . \gamma_{t}=\log\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\alpha_{t}^{2}},\qquad\frac{\alpha_{t}^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}={\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma_{t}}. (26) 
For fixed ( γ s , γ t ) (\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t}) , the per-level objective of 18 reads 
𝒦 s , t ( ϕ ) = KL ( q t ϕ , s ∥ q t ) = 𝔼 𝐞 s , ϵ [ log q t ϕ , s ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s ) − log q t ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s ) ] . \mathcal{K}_{s,t}(\phi)=\operatorname{KL}\big(q_{t}^{\phi,s}\,\|\,q_{t}\big)=\E_{{\mathbf{e}}_{s},{\bm{\epsilon}}}\Big[\log q_{t}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s})-\log q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s})\Big]. (27) 
There are two contributions when differentiating the student
log-density: its explicit dependence on ϕ \phi at a fixed input,
and its dependence through the sampled input 𝐞 t ϕ , s {\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s} .
Consequently, 
∇ ϕ 𝒦 s , t \displaystyle\nabla_{\phi}\mathcal{K}_{s,t} = 𝔼 𝐞 s , ϵ ⁡ [ ∇ ϕ ​ log ​ q t ϕ , s ​ ( 𝐞 ) | 𝐞 = 𝐞 t ϕ , s + ( ∂ 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∂ ϕ ) ⊤ ​ ( ∇ 𝐞 ​ log ​ q t ϕ , s ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s ) − ∇ 𝐞 ​ log ​ q t ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s ) ) ] . \displaystyle=\E_{{\mathbf{e}}_{s},{\bm{\epsilon}}}\Big[\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{t}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}})\Big|_{{\mathbf{e}}={\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s}}+\Big(\frac{\partial{\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s}}{\partial\phi}\Big)^{\!\top}\Big(\nabla_{{\mathbf{e}}}\log q_{t}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s})-\nabla_{{\mathbf{e}}}\log q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s})\Big)\Big]. (28) 
The expectation of the explicit density derivative vanishes: 
𝔼 𝐞 ∼ q t ϕ , s ⁡ [ ∇ ϕ ​ log ​ q t ϕ , s ​ ( 𝐞 ) | 𝐞 ​ fixed ] \displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{e}}\sim q_{t}^{\phi,s}}\Big[\nabla_{\phi}\log q_{t}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}})\Big|_{{\mathbf{e}}\ \mathrm{fixed}}\Big] = ∫ ∇ ϕ q t ϕ , s ( 𝐞 ) d 𝐞 = ∇ ϕ ∫ q t ϕ , s ( 𝐞 ) d 𝐞 = 0 . \displaystyle=\int\nabla_{\phi}q_{t}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}})\,d{\mathbf{e}}=\nabla_{\phi}\int q_{t}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}})\,d{\mathbf{e}}=0. (29) 
Thus, introducing the student and target scores 
𝐬 stu ϕ , s ​ ( 𝐞 , γ t ) := ∇ 𝐞 ​ log ​ q t ϕ , s ​ ( 𝐞 ) , 𝐬 ref ​ ( 𝐞 , γ t ) := ∇ 𝐞 ​ log ​ q t ​ ( 𝐞 ) , \mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{stu}}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}},\gamma_{t}):=\nabla_{{\mathbf{e}}}\log q_{t}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}}),\qquad\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{ref}}({\mathbf{e}},\gamma_{t}):=\nabla_{{\mathbf{e}}}\log q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}), (30) 
we obtain 
∇ ϕ 𝒦 s , t = 𝔼 𝐞 s , ϵ ⁡ [ ( ∂ 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∂ ϕ ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝐬 stu ϕ , s ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) − 𝐬 ref ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ) ] . \nabla_{\phi}\mathcal{K}_{s,t}=\E_{{\mathbf{e}}_{s},{\bm{\epsilon}}}\Big[\Big(\frac{\partial{\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s}}{\partial\phi}\Big)^{\!\top}\Big(\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{stu}}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})-\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{ref}}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})\Big)\Big]. (31) 
For Gaussian corruption, the score is related to the posterior
mean of the clean embedding by 
∇ 𝐞 t ​ log ​ q t ​ ( 𝐞 t ) = α t ​ 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝐞 0 ∣ 𝐞 t ] − 𝐞 t σ t 2 . \nabla_{{\mathbf{e}}_{t}}\log q_{t}({\mathbf{e}}_{t})=\frac{\alpha_{t}\,\E[{\mathbf{e}}_{0}\mid{\mathbf{e}}_{t}]-{\mathbf{e}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}. (32) 
Let the ideal auxiliary and reference denoisers be 
𝐞 η ⋆ ( 𝐞 t , γ t ) := 𝔼 stu [ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∣ 𝐞 t , γ s , γ t ] , 𝐞 ψ ⋆ ( 𝐞 t , γ t ) := 𝔼 ref [ 𝐞 0 ∣ 𝐞 t , γ t ] , {\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t}):=\E_{\mathrm{stu}}[{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}\mid{\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t}],\qquad{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t}):=\E_{\mathrm{ref}}[{\mathbf{e}}_{0}\mid{\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t}], (33) 
where source conditioning of the ideal auxiliary is suppressed
in its arguments.
The corresponding score difference is therefore 
𝐬 stu ϕ , s ​ ( 𝐞 t , γ t ) − 𝐬 ref ​ ( 𝐞 t , γ t ) \displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{stu}}^{\phi,s}({\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t})-\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{ref}}({\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t}) = α t ​ 𝐞 η ⋆ ​ ( 𝐞 t , γ t ) − 𝐞 t σ t 2 − α t ​ 𝐞 ψ ⋆ ​ ( 𝐞 t , γ t ) − 𝐞 t σ t 2 \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{t}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{\alpha_{t}{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}} 
= α t σ t 2 ​ [ 𝐞 η ⋆ ​ ( 𝐞 t , γ t ) − 𝐞 ψ ⋆ ​ ( 𝐞 t , γ t ) ] . \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{t}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Big[{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t},\gamma_{t})\Big]. (34) 
Meanwhile, because the corruption noise and detached source are
held fixed under differentiation, 
∂ 𝐞 t ϕ , s ∂ ϕ = α t ​ ∂ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∂ ϕ . \frac{\partial{\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s}}{\partial\phi}=\alpha_{t}\frac{\partial{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}}{\partial\phi}. (35) 
Combining these identities gives 
∇ ϕ 𝒦 s , t \displaystyle\nabla_{\phi}\mathcal{K}_{s,t} = 𝔼 𝐞 s , ϵ ⁡ [ α t 2 σ t 2 ​ ( ∂ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∂ ϕ ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝐞 η ⋆ ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) − 𝐞 ψ ⋆ ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ) ] \displaystyle=\E_{{\mathbf{e}}_{s},{\bm{\epsilon}}}\Big[\frac{\alpha_{t}^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Big(\frac{\partial{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}}{\partial\phi}\Big)^{\!\top}\Big({\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})\Big)\Big] 
= 𝔼 𝐞 s , ϵ ⁡ [ e − γ t ​ ( ∂ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∂ ϕ ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝐞 η ⋆ ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) − 𝐞 ψ ⋆ ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ) ] . \displaystyle=\E_{{\mathbf{e}}_{s},{\bm{\epsilon}}}\Big[{\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma_{t}}\Big(\frac{\partial{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}}{\partial\phi}\Big)^{\!\top}\Big({\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}^{\star}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})\Big)\Big]. (36) 
Replacing the ideal posterior means with learned denoisers and
averaging over source and query levels as in 18 yields 
∇ ϕ ℒ DMD ϕ = 𝔼 γ s , γ t , 𝐞 s , ϵ ⁡ [ e − γ t ​ ( ∂ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s ∂ ϕ ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝐞 η ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) − 𝐞 ψ ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ) ] . \nabla_{\phi}\mathcal{L}^{\phi}_{\text{DMD}}=\E_{\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t},{\mathbf{e}}_{s},{\bm{\epsilon}}}\Big[{\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma_{t}}\Big(\frac{\partial{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s}}{\partial\phi}\Big)^{\!\top}\Big({\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})\Big)\Big]. (37) 
The Jacobian–vector product above contracts over both the L ′ L^{\prime} token
positions of the block and the d e d_{e} embedding coordinates, so writing it
as a sum of per-token inner products shows that this gradient is obtained
by differentiating the scalar surrogate 
ℒ DMD-grad ϕ = 𝔼 γ s , γ t , 𝐞 s , ϵ ⁡ [ e − γ t ​ ∑ l = 1 L ′ ⟨ 𝐞 0 ϕ , s , l , sg ⁡ [ 𝐞 η l ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) − 𝐞 ψ l ​ ( 𝐞 t ϕ , s , γ t ) ] ⟩ ] , \mathcal{L}^{\phi}_{\text{DMD-grad}}=\E_{\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t},{\mathbf{e}}_{s},{\bm{\epsilon}}}\Big[{\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma_{t}}\sum_{l=1}^{L^{\prime}}\Big\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{0}^{\phi,s,l},\operatorname{sg}\big[{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta}^{l}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})-{\mathbf{e}}_{\psi}^{l}({\mathbf{e}}_{t}^{\phi,s},\gamma_{t})\big]\Big\rangle\Big]\,, (38) 
which is 19 and concludes the proof.
∎ 
Appendix G Implementation Details and Additional Results for Supervised Fine-Tuning 
[Back to Sec. 7 in the main paper.] 
G.1 Supervised Fine-Tuning Setup 
Our SFT pipeline uses NeMo-RL with joint diffusion and AR supervision on assistant responses, following Nemotron-Labs-Diffusion (NLD) ( Fu et al., 2026 ) . Prompts remain clean, and training and evaluation share the non-thinking chat boundary described below.
We freeze the diffusion embedding 𝐄 {\mathbf{E}} , noise-schedule network γ ~ ψ ​ ( t ) \tilde{\gamma}_{\psi}(t) , and endpoints γ 0 , γ 1 \gamma_{0},\gamma_{1} . The clean-stream input embedding, transformer, conditioning projections, and both readout heads remain trainable. Therefore, SUSReg is used only during pretraining. Initialization and objective. 
We initialize from the 150k-step pretrained Sigma - unreg -3B/8B, Sigma - preg -3B/8B, and Sigma -3B/8B checkpoints.
Before SFT of Sigma - preg -8B and Sigma -8B, we reinitialize the AR embedding rows with ℓ 2 \ell_{2} norm below 10 − 6 10^{-6} to the mean direction of the remaining rows, scaled to their median norm. This includes chat-boundary IDs 10 and 11 and prevents the initial gradient-norm transient caused by their near-zero embeddings. The repair is applied once; its benchmark effect is not separately ablated.
Let 𝒜 \mathcal{A} denote the supervised answer positions, including the terminal EOS padding described below. The SFT objective is 
ℒ sft = α ​ ∑ i ∈ 𝒜 ℒ BD , i | 𝒜 | + ∑ i ∈ 𝒜 ℒ ar , i | 𝒜 | , \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{sft}}=\alpha\,\frac{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{BD},i}}{|\mathcal{A}|}+\frac{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{ar},i}}{|\mathcal{A}|}, (39) 
where ℒ BD \mathcal{L}_{\text{BD}} is the continuous block NELBO of 3 and 4 , including its diffusion, reconstruction, and prior terms, and ℒ ar \mathcal{L}_{\text{ar}} is next-token cross-entropy from the clean stream 6 . The ELBO weight is α = 0.3 \alpha=0.3 for Sigma - unreg -3B/8B and Sigma - preg -3B/8B, and α = 1 \alpha=1 for Sigma -3B/8B, following pretraining ( App. C ). Numerators and token counts are reduced globally across data-parallel ranks. The AR and diffusion denominators coincide under the answer mask because position zero is unsupervised, so α \alpha has the same meaning as the pretraining diffusion-loss weight. 
As in pretraining ( Sec. 3.2 ), we estimate the reconstruction and diffusion terms with the Horvitz–Thompson estimator in 5 : independently for each block, we select reconstruction at t = 0 t=0 with probability p rec = 0.1 p_{\mathrm{rec}}=0.1 and otherwise draw t ∼ Unif ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] t\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1] . Gaussian noise is applied only in embedding space; the token IDs always remain clean. Prompt and ordinary batch-padding positions have zero loss and are pinned to their clean embeddings, whereas the synthetic EOS padding is supervised. Answer blocks see the clean context 𝐜 = ( 𝐩 , 𝐫 ( < b ) ) {\mathbf{c}}=({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{r}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0.4297,0.2148}<b})}) of Sec. 4.1 . Data and preprocessing. 
We use the same non-thinking, single-turn SFT corpus as NLD ( Chandiramani et al., 2026 ) : 848,087 848{,}087 training and 1,200 1{,}200 validation conversations, each with an empty system message, a user prompt, and an assistant response.
We use the same Mistral-NeMo ( Mistral AI, 2024 ) tokenizer ( V = 131,072 V=131{,}072 ) and the Nemotron Nano chat template ( Blakeman et al., 2025 ) , explicitly setting enable_thinking=false . Thus, the supervised response follows the intended prompt suffix <think></think> ; training and evaluation use the same answer boundary.
During training, we left-pad prompts with at most 31 31 unsupervised tokens so answers begin on the 32 32 -token block grid; no padding is inserted between prompt and answer. Inference uses unpadded, fully causal prompts. Supervised EOS tokens complete the final answer block, with one full EOS block appended when already aligned. Unsupervised right-padding then rounds the batch length to a multiple of 1024 1024 to limit FlexAttention compilation shapes. Samples exceeding 8192 8192 tokens after padding are dropped. Optimization. 
Both model sizes train for 8000 8000 steps ( ≈ 2.4 \approx 2.4 epochs) at sequence length and maximum position 8192 8192 , block length 32 32 , and global/micro batch sizes 256 / 1 256/1 . We use AdamW with ( β 1 , β 2 ) = ( 0.9 , 0.98 ) (\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0.9,0.98) , ϵ = 10 − 5 \epsilon=10^{-5} , weight decay 0.1 0.1 , and learning rate 2.5 × 10 − 6 2.5\times 10^{-6} after 50 50 steps of linear warmup, held constant thereafter. Training uses BF16 , gradient clipping at 1.0 1.0 , and FSDP2 with TP = {}={} CP = {}={} 1, activation checkpointing, and no sequence packing. Self-conditioning is enabled for 25 % 25\% of microbatches on every optimizer step and all validation sequences. The chat template enables the generation prompt; clean-prompt, answer-only supervision and EOS padding follow the preprocessing above. 
Setting the maximum position to 8192 8192 also extends the pretrained rotary frequencies: the effective YaRN factor is 8192 / 16384 = 0.5 8192/16384=0.5 , compared with 0.25 0.25 at the pretraining length of 4096 4096 . 
G.2 Evaluation Setup, CFG–ST Sweep, and Full Results 
The instruct evaluations in Tabs. 4 and 5 use the base-model harness ( Sec. E.3 ), with the differences below. Benchmarks. 
We evaluate GSM8K (1319 problems), MATH-500 (500), AIME 2024/2025 (30 each), HumanEval+ (164), MBPP (378), LiveCodeBench-CPP (454), MMLU (14042), GPQA (198), and IFEval (541), using the SFT chat template and diffusion block length L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 . Scoring. 
Math tasks use symbolic equivalence on the final \boxed{} answer; GSM8K also accepts the last number. HumanEval and MBPP report executed pass@ 1 1 on EvalPlus’s base tests ( passing_base_tests ), and LiveCodeBench-CPP compiles and tests extracted C++ programs. GPQA and MMLU request reasoning ending in Answer: \boxed{A/B/C/D} ; grading extracts the last boxed letter, with a regex fallback. IFEval reports instruction-level loose accuracy, matching the reference models in Tabs. 4 and 5 . Baselines. 
Baseline scores come from Fu et al. (2026, Tabs. 5, 7) , except NLD-8B diffusion, which we reproduce under the aligned protocol without format injection. We use SDAR-8B Chat at TPF = 1.00 =1.00 rather than 1.75 1.75 : it scores higher on eight tasks, with gaps ≤ 0.6 \leq 0.6 points on the remaining two (MMLU and HumanEval). Decoding algorithms. 
As in the evaluation for base models, we always enable SC; DDPM-256 uses EDM ρ = 7 \rho=7 schedule, and DDIM-32 uses uniform- γ \gamma schedule. Their respective ( w , τ ) (w,\tau) settings follow the recommended values for base models Tab. 10 . Generation budget. 
The generation budget is 1024 1024 tokens for GSM8K and HumanEval at 3B, rising to 1536 1536 and 2560 2560 , respectively, at 8B; 2560 2560 for MBPP, LiveCodeBench-CPP, and IFEval; and 4096 4096 for AIME 2024/2025, MATH-500, and GPQA. MMLU uses 2048 2048 for 3B DDPM and 2560 2560 otherwise. AR uses 16384 16384 except on MMLU ( 2560 2560 ). Caps follow per-lane graded-answer lengths: no correct answer in the smoke runs required more than 4096 4096 tokens, and the longer tail consists mainly of degenerate repetition. Seed error bars. 
Each diffusion row averages 10 10 independent sampling lanes and reports half-width 2 10 ​ σ ^ \frac{2}{\sqrt{10}}\hat{\sigma} , where σ ^ \hat{\sigma} is the standard deviation of per-lane benchmark scores. AR greedily decodes the clean stream through its AR head. Post-SFT CFG–ST sweep. 
On GSM8K and HumanEval, the pre-SFT guidance and temperature optima remain within sampling uncertainty of the best post-SFT settings for both 3B checkpoints and samplers ( Tab. 20 ). Appendix H Implementation Details and Additional Results for Self-Speculative Decoding 
This section gives the protocol ( Sec. H.1 ) and all results:
the models of Sec. 6 in full ( Sec. H.2 ),
the undistilled Sigma with trained drafters ( Sec. H.3 ),
and acceptance training ( Sec. H.4 ). 
H.1 Setup Drafting and verification. 
Following the two-pass framework of NLD ( Fu et al., 2026 , Sec. 3.3) , each round initializes one block of L ′ = 32 L^{\prime}=32 draft positions with Gaussian noise in the 16-dimensional latent space and reads out categorical draft tokens from a single denoiser call at t = 1 t=1 .
The AR head then scores all drafts causally in one forward pass over the shared backbone and cached prefix, keeps the longest prefix that matches its own greedy tokens, and appends one token of its own.
Draft-only parameters are disabled during verification, so the committed text is the model’s greedy AR output, up to BF16 near-ties between the decoding and verification kernels.
More denoiser calls per draft raised AL but not tokens per forward pass, so we use one. Trained drafters. 
Optionally, we train draft-only parameters on Tulu-3 prompts ( Lambert et al., 2024 ) with NLD’s LK-hybrid and verifier cross-entropy objective ( Fu et al., 2026 , Eqs. 6–9) , keeping the backbone and the AR head frozen.
We train a rank-128 LoRA on the query and value projections together with the diffusion head ( Q/V , 440M parameters at 3B and 595M at 8B).
At 8B, the 6 of 1,000 training steps whose gradient norm is not finite make no update. Benchmarks. 
GSM8K (1,319 problems, 8-shot chain of thought), HumanEval (164), and MBPP (500) use the prompts of our base-model evaluation.
The end-of-text token is ignored, and an answer ends at the first of the task’s stop strings, within 256, 512, and 512 new tokens. Serving. 
We serve with SGLang ( Zheng et al., 2024 ) in BF16 , with greedy decoding, TP1, and one request at a time on one NVIDIA GB200.
AL and accuracy use the numerics of the training code (FP32 diffusion-stream parameters and eager kernels).
Throughput uses FlashAttention-4 and CUDA graphs. NLD reference. 
NLD-3B-Base and NLD-8B-Base ship without a drafter adapter, so they draft with their own diffusion head.
Every NLD model runs at its released block size: 32 positions holding the last committed token and 31 drafts, one draft fewer per round than Sigma .
NLD’s speculative server searched only the end of each committed block for stop strings and missed earlier ones; we let it search the whole block, as SGLang’s own speculative path does, so that it stops in the block that contains the first stop.
NLD’s runner times AR and speculative decoding once each, concurrently on two GPUs of one node. Metrics. 
AL counts the draft tokens that a round adds to the returned answer, i.e., its committed answer tokens minus the verifier’s own token, pooled over rounds; drafts accepted past the end of the answer are discarded and not counted.
Repetition loops are trivially predictable and would inflate AL.
On GSM8K, HumanEval, and MBPP, we therefore drop, per model, every prompt whose AR, speculative, or timing output loops (at least half of its 16-grams repeat) or reaches the length cap; accuracy uses all prompts.
Throughput is answer tokens per second of client time, and speedup divides the speculative throughput by the same model’s AR throughput on the same GPU and prompts, averaged over two runs on different GPUs.
Paired gains use the prompts that both models keep, with 95% percentile bootstrap intervals over prompts.

H.3 Undistilled Model and Trained Drafters 
The undistilled Sigma -3B and Sigma -8B can draft with their own diffusion head or with a trained Q/V adapter ( Tab. 24 ). 
Distillation helps more than a trained drafter at 3B. The adapter raises Sigma -3B’s AL by 13.1%, 5.4%, and 7.2% on GSM8K, HumanEval, and MBPP ([12.2, 14.0], [3.1, 7.6], and [5.8, 8.9]).
The distilled student, with its own head and no adapter, gains 21.8%, 20.1%, and 15.0% over Sigma -3B ([20.1, 23.5], [8.6, 31.7], and [11.8, 18.1]). 
At 8B, the two are closer. The adapter raises Sigma -8B’s AL by 16.1%, 9.4%, and 14.3% ([15.1, 17.1], [6.1, 13.0], and [12.4, 16.1]), and distillation by 22.4%, 7.9%, and 19.9% ([20.9, 24.0], [ − - 0.5, 16.8], and [16.7, 23.2]).
Compared directly, Sigma - distill -8B accepts 5.4% and 4.9% more than Sigma -8B with the adapter on GSM8K and MBPP ([4.0, 6.8] and [2.0, 7.7]) and 1.6% fewer on HumanEval ([ − - 9.3, 6.4]), where it also decodes slower (2.37 × \times against 2.73 × \times ). 
The adapter leaves the output unchanged. It is disabled during verification, so Sigma -3B and Sigma -8B solve exactly the same problems with and without it. H.4 Acceptance Training of the Distilled Model 
Instead of training a separate drafter, we continue the distillation with an acceptance objective that updates the whole backbone; we call the result Sigma - distill - spec .
Let ϕ \phi denote the parameters of the student, which starts from Sigma - distill , and ϕ ¯ \bar{\phi} those of a frozen copy of Sigma - distill .
We keep the distillation objective and add two terms.
An LK-hybrid loss aligns the student’s one-call draft with the AR head of ϕ ¯ \bar{\phi} , and a KL term holds the student’s AR distribution to that of ϕ ¯ \bar{\phi} , so that the verifier, and hence the decoded output, is preserved: 
ℒ acc = λ DMD ​ ℒ DMD + λ LK ​ ℒ LK + λ ^ ret ​ ℒ ret . \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{acc}}=\lambda_{\text{DMD}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{DMD}}+\lambda_{\text{LK}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{LK}}+\hat{\lambda}_{\text{ret}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{ret}}. (40) 
Training updates the shared transformer and the diffusion head; as in distillation ( Sec. F.2 ), the embedding matrix 𝐄 {\mathbf{E}} , the noise schedule, and the AR head stay frozen. Distribution matching. 
ℒ DMD \mathcal{L}_{\text{DMD}} is the student objective of App. F ( Alg. 3 ), with the auxiliary and reference denoisers and the setting of the distillation that produced Sigma - distill : two phases, no self-conditioning, and segments that start from fresh student rollouts.
Acceptance training thus continues that distillation. Acceptance. 
Each LK example takes a context 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} from the generations of an earlier 3B DMD student on math and code prompts, mostly GSM8K- and HumanEval-style, and drafts the next block with one call from pure noise, as in serving ( Sec. H.1 ).
Both sizes use these contexts; the two runs averaged into each Sigma - distill - spec model use two versions of the context set.
Test and dev problems are excluded by exact match; a 13-gram scan finds 3, 1, and 54 near-duplicates of GSM8K, HumanEval, and MBPP test problems among the contexts, and excluding those problems leaves the AL gains of acceptance training unchanged.
Slot j ∈ { 1 , … , L ′ } j\in\{1,\ldots,L^{\prime}\} has draft distribution p ^ j := 𝐱 ϕ j ​ ( 𝐳 1 , 1 , 𝐜 ) \hat{p}^{j}:={\mathbf{x}}^{j}_{\phi}({\mathbf{z}}_{1},1;{\mathbf{c}}) with 𝐳 1 ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 𝟎 , 𝐈 ) {\mathbf{z}}_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}({\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{I}}) and draft token v ^ j := argmax v p ^ j ​ ( v ) \hat{v}^{j}:=\argmax_{v}\hat{p}^{j}(v) .
The verifier reads all slots in one causal pass and gives p j := 𝐱 ϕ ¯ AR | 𝐜 | + j ​ ( 𝐱 ~ < | 𝐜 | + j ) p^{j}:={\mathbf{x}}^{|{\mathbf{c}}|+j}_{\bar{\phi}_{\text{AR}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{<|{\mathbf{c}}|+j}) , where 𝐱 ~ \tilde{\mathbf{x}} is 𝐜 {\mathbf{c}} followed by the draft tokens.
If the first a a draft tokens equal the verifier’s greedy tokens v ⋆ j := argmax v p j ​ ( v ) v^{j}_{\star}:=\argmax_{v}p^{j}(v) and slot a + 1 a+1 does not, the loss covers the accepted prefix and the first rejected slot, 𝒜 = { 1 , … , min ⁡ ( a + 1 , L ′ ) } \mathcal{A}=\{1,\ldots,\min(a+1,L^{\prime})\} .
Following NLD ( Fu et al., 2026 , Eqs. 6–9) , let U j U^{j} be the union of the 200 most probable tokens under p j p^{j} and under p ^ j \hat{p}^{j} ; let p τ j p^{j}_{\tau} and p ^ τ j \hat{p}^{j}_{\tau} be p j p^{j} and p ^ j \hat{p}^{j} restricted to U j U^{j} and renormalized at temperature τ LK = 3 \tau_{\text{LK}}=3 , i.e., p τ j ​ ( v ) ∝ p j ​ ( v ) 1 / τ LK p^{j}_{\tau}(v)\propto p^{j}(v)^{1/\tau_{\text{LK}}} for v ∈ U j v\in U^{j} ; and let p ^ 1 j \hat{p}^{j}_{1} be the same restriction of p ^ j \hat{p}^{j} at temperature 1. Then 
ℒ LK = 𝔼 [ 1 | 𝒜 | ∑ j ∈ 𝒜 ν j KL ( p τ j ∥ p ^ τ j ) + ( 1 − ν j ) TV ( p τ j , p ^ τ j ) − log p ^ 1 j ( v ⋆ j ) ] , ν j = exp ( − η sg ( ω j ) ) , \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{LK}}=\E\Big[\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{A}|}{\textstyle\sum}_{j\in\mathcal{A}}\nu^{j}\,\operatorname{KL}(p^{j}_{\tau}\,\|\,\hat{p}^{j}_{\tau})+(1-\nu^{j})\,\mathrm{TV}(p^{j}_{\tau},\hat{p}^{j}_{\tau})-\log\hat{p}^{j}_{1}(v^{j}_{\star})\Big],\qquad\nu^{j}=\exp\!\big(-\eta\,\operatorname{sg}(\omega^{j})\big), (41) 
where ω j = ∑ v ∈ U j min ⁡ ( p τ j ​ ( v ) , p ^ τ j ​ ( v ) ) = 1 − TV ⁡ ( p τ j , p ^ τ j ) \omega^{j}={\textstyle\sum}_{v\in U^{j}}\min\big(p^{j}_{\tau}(v),\hat{p}^{j}_{\tau}(v)\big)=1-\mathrm{TV}(p^{j}_{\tau},\hat{p}^{j}_{\tau}) is the probability that a token sampled from p ^ τ j \hat{p}^{j}_{\tau} is accepted, and η = 0.5 \eta=0.5 ; the weight thus shifts from the KL term toward total variation as the draft approaches the verifier.
The retention term is the forward KL from the AR distribution of ϕ ¯ \bar{\phi} to that of the student, averaged over the set ℛ \mathcal{R} of the last 128 positions of 𝐱 ~ \tilde{\mathbf{x}} , i.e., the L ′ L^{\prime} draft slots and the 96 context tokens before them: 
ℒ ret = 𝔼 [ 1 | ℛ | ∑ l ∈ ℛ KL ( 𝐱 ϕ ¯ AR l ( 𝐱 ~ < l ) ∥ 𝐱 ϕ AR l ( 𝐱 ~ < l ) ) ] . \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{ret}}=\E\Big[\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}{\textstyle\sum}_{l\in\mathcal{R}}\operatorname{KL}\big({\mathbf{x}}^{l}_{\bar{\phi}_{\text{AR}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{<l})\,\big\|\,{\mathbf{x}}^{l}_{\phi_{\text{AR}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{<l})\big)\Big]. (42) Weights and optimization. 
We set λ LK = 0.25 \lambda_{\text{LK}}=0.25 and λ ret = 12 \lambda_{\text{ret}}=12 , and cap the retention weight at 
λ ^ ret = min ⁡ ( λ ret , 3 ​ ‖ ∇ ϕ ( λ DMD ​ ℒ DMD + λ LK ​ ℒ LK ) ‖ ‖ ∇ ϕ ℒ ret ‖ ) , \displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{\text{ret}}=\min\Big(\lambda_{\text{ret}},\;3\,\frac{\|\nabla_{\phi}(\lambda_{\text{DMD}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{DMD}}+\lambda_{\text{LK}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{LK}})\|}{\|\nabla_{\phi}\mathcal{L}_{\text{ret}}\|}\Big), (43) 
with both gradient norms taken from the previous update.
Without the cap, once the AR distribution drifted, the retention gradient grew until it dominated the clipped update, and training diverged.
Each update draws 64 DMD examples and 128 drafts.
The drafts form chains of four rounds: the first round drafts after a prefix of up to 1,664 tokens that keeps the prompt’s BOS token, and each later round drafts after the tokens that the previous round committed, as in serving.
The optimizers are those of Sec. F.2 , except that the student learning rate is doubled to 7.2 × 10 − 7 7.2\times 10^{-7} ; the drafts never use self-conditioning, as in serving.
Each Sigma - distill - spec model is a uniform weight average of two runs with λ DMD = 1 \lambda_{\text{DMD}}=1 and 0.5 0.5 , each trained for 600 updates from Sigma - distill while the learning rate decays linearly to zero.
At 8B, the teacher-generated DMD data has 16,384 contexts instead of 65,536, and the fit of the query noise levels and the LK contexts are reused from 3B. Results. 
Table 25 gives the gains.
Acceptance training raises AL by 5.2%, 6.0%, and 6.1% at 3B and by 8.5%, 4.8%, and 8.1% at 8B on GSM8K, HumanEval, and MBPP; the HumanEval intervals include zero at both sizes.
It leaves AR accuracy unchanged on all three benchmarks at both sizes (sign tests, p ≥ 0.10 p\geq 0.10 at 3B and p ≥ 0.69 p\geq 0.69 at 8B).
